Reading, Writing, Sharing, and Interacting
with the World through Video
A Survey Toward Unified Video World Models
Shengqiong Wu1,∗, Meng Luo2,∗, Bobo Li2, Ziwei Liu3,
Shuicheng Yan2, Ming-Hsuan Yang4, Philip Torr1, Hao Fei1,♠
1University of Oxford
2NUS
3NTU
4University of California, Merced
∗Equal contribution
♠Project lead, correspondence (haofei7419@gmail.com)
Project: https://vid-gen.github.io/video-world-model-survey
GitHub: https://github.com/vid-gen/awesome-video-world-model
Abstract
Video foundation models have advanced rapidly in understanding and generating visual
sequences, while video world models seek to capture the underlying dynamics of the world
they depict. Despite their different objectives, both concern the relationship between video
observations and an evolving world. This survey adopts world modeling as a unifying lens for
examining these developments. Viewing an observed video as a partial, lossy, and temporally
ordered projection of a latent temporal world, we organize the literature around four com-
plementary operations:
Y reading infers world properties from observed video;
Ò writing
synthesizes video from specified or learned world conditions;
T sharing integrates understand-
ing and generation through common modeling mechanisms; and
 interacting predicts and
updates world states under actions and evolving observations. This perspective distinguishes
temporal coherence in observed or generated sequences from consistency in the underlying
world state. Across tasks, representations, models, datasets, and benchmarks, we trace a
shift in emphasis from clip-level recognition and perceptual fidelity toward persistent state,
controllable evolution, physical and causal validity, and evaluation grounded in interaction
and downstream decisions, and we distill the open challenges that follow.

5


Video Foundation Models
World-Modeling Lens
1
Introduction
“Vision is the process of discovering from images what is present in the world, and where it is.”
David Marr, Vision, 1982 [338]
Video occupies a distinctive position among the modalities available to machine intelligence. Unlike static
images that capture individual moments, text that conveys selectively described information, or audio
that primarily records acoustic dynamics, video directly captures how visible entities, their relations, and
actions evolve across space and time, often together with synchronized audio, speech, and text. This rich
spatiotemporal information makes video essential to a wide range of applications, including communication,
entertainment, autonomous driving, robotics, healthcare, and education. Consequently, video has long been a
central subject of computer vision and multimedia research, progressing from spatiotemporal architectures
for action recognition [39, 102, 208] to self-supervised pretraining for transferable video representations [453,
482]. More recently, advances in large language models (LLMs) and large-scale generative modeling have
driven a shift from task-specific video models toward general-purpose video foundation models, expanding
their capabilities along three complementary directions. For video understanding, Video-LLMs such as
Video-LLaVA [271] enable language-based interaction with visual sequences, while Qwen3-VL [12] extends
multimodal context to 256K tokens to accommodate longer video inputs. For video generation, models
such as Sora [31], Wan [462], HunyuanVideo [219], Movie Gen [372], and Seedance 2.0 [442] demonstrate
increasingly capable synthesis under diverse conditions, with Sora and Loong [487] further exploring minute-
scale generation. Toward the unification of understanding and generation, emerging models [479, 498, 531]
seek to integrate these capabilities through shared token representations, latent spaces, or modeling interfaces,
bringing video perception and synthesis into a common foundation-model framework.
Beyond video understanding and generation, a broader research direction is emerging around modeling how
the physical world evolves, rather than merely interpreting or synthesizing its visual observations. Although
language models acquire extensive knowledge through text, their representations are only indirectly grounded
in physical experience, motivating the pursuit of world models that can predict how an environment changes
over time and support reasoning and planning [59, 229]. This idea has roots in model-based reinforcement
learning, where agents learn predictive dynamics to simulate possible futures and evaluate candidate actions
before acting [143, 146]. Video provides a natural and scalable foundation for extending this paradigm beyond
task-specific environments: by capturing continuous changes in objects, relations, and actions, large-scale
video data offers rich supervision for learning world dynamics without requiring manually annotated labels,
although actions and underlying physical states often remain unobserved. Building on this opportunity, recent
research has explored several complementary approaches to video-based world modeling. Generative models
such as Sora [31] have motivated the prospect of learning world dynamics through video synthesis, while
Genie [32] demonstrates how interactive environments can be learned from unlabeled videos. Action- and
condition-aware models such as GAIA-1 [172] and Cosmos [355] extend predictive generation toward driving
and physical AI, whereas V-JEPA 2 [9] and Dreamer 4 [147] explore predictive representations and imagined
trajectories for planning and control. Together with progress in interactive simulation [561], egocentric and
robotic learning [133, 511], and long-horizon prediction [528], these developments are expanding the role of
video from a medium for perception and generation toward a foundation for predicting and interacting with
dynamic environments. Figure 1 traces milestones of these lines of work.
These two lines of progress are increasingly converging around a common challenge: connecting video
observations with the dynamics of the world they depict. Video world models must infer underlying states
from observed sequences before predicting how those states evolve, and often translate predicted states
back into visual observations for simulation, evaluation, or planning.
Conversely, video generators are
increasingly incorporating action- and state-dependent controls, while unified video models explicitly couple
visual understanding with synthesis. Despite their different objectives, these approaches therefore share a
fundamental question: what relationship does a model learn between an observed video and the evolving world
that gives rise to it?
To examine this question systematically, we adopt world modeling as a unifying perspective on video foundation
models. Specifically, we view an observed video V as a partial, lossy, and temporally ordered observation
6


Video Foundation Models
World-Modeling Lens
of an underlying latent temporal world W. This distinction provides a common basis for organizing four
complementary research paradigms:
Y reading, which infers world properties from observed video;
Ò writing,
which synthesizes video observations from specified or learned world conditions;
T sharing, which integrates
both directions within a unified modeling framework; and
 interacting, which updates or predicts world
states in response to actions and evolving observations. These paradigms characterize different ways in which
models connect video observations to the underlying world, rather than merely categorizing models by their
input and output modalities. This perspective enables a systematic comparison of their objectives, capabilities,
and evaluation criteria while preserving the distinctions between visual plausibility, temporal coherence, and
consistency of the modeled world. Section 2 formalizes this framework and clarifies how these properties differ
across the four paradigms.
Existing relevant surveys have examined related developments from different perspectives, including general
world models organized around predictive state representations across modalities [84], interactive video world
models centered on online prediction and control [286, 320], 3D and 4D world modeling grounded in explicit
geometric representations [218], multimodal language models emphasizing the integration of visual encoders
and language reasoning [581], long-form video generation focusing on temporal extension and consistency [240],
and audio-visual foundation models spanning complementary modalities [384]. While these surveys provide
valuable insights into individual research directions, they do not collectively establish a unified account of how
video models connect observations to evolving world states across understanding, generation, unification, and
interaction. Our survey addresses this gap by taking the relation between video observations and the underlying
dynamic world as its central organizing principle, not any single architecture, task, or modality.
Under this perspective, we systematically examine the four paradigms of reading, writing, sharing, and
interacting through a common analytical framework. For each paradigm, we review the tasks, datasets, and
evaluation protocols that define its objectives; the representations and model architectures that support its
capabilities; the reasoning and control mechanisms that extend its functionality; and the applications that
demonstrate its practical utility. Beyond reviewing individual paradigms, we identify several cross-cutting
questions: how models represent and preserve world states beyond immediate observations, how actions
and external conditions influence state evolution, and how predictive capabilities translate into effective
reasoning and decision-making. These questions also motivate a closer examination of evaluation practices,
particularly the distinction between conventional metrics of recognition accuracy or perceptual fidelity and
emerging criteria concerning state persistence, controllability, physical and causal consistency, and downstream
utility. Within the interacting paradigm, we further distinguish video world models along two complementary
dimensions: prediction space, ranging from observation-space to latent-state prediction, and transition
interface, ranging from passive prediction to action-conditioned rollout and closed-loop interaction. This
taxonomy separates what a model predicts from how its predictions are conditioned, updated, and used.
Our review covers publicly available research that contributes relevant architectural, methodological, or
evaluation evidence. We retain foundational studies where necessary to establish conceptual and technical
developments, while interpreting recent advances in the context of their specific tasks, training data, and
evaluation protocols. Throughout the survey, we distinguish between the capabilities supported by a model’s
design, the supervision provided by its training data, and the empirical evidence demonstrated by its evaluation,
avoiding assumptions that strong performance on individual video tasks necessarily implies consistent or
generalizable world modeling.
Specifically, this survey makes three main contributions:
A world-modeling lens. The four-operation framework of reading, writing, sharing, and interacting relates
video understanding, video generation, unified video models, and video world models in one account, plus a
two-axis classification of world models by prediction space and transition interface.
A comprehensive scope with insights. Across tasks, representations, models, systems, datasets, and
benchmarks, we compare how the four operations represent state, learn from video, accept control, preserve
memory, and are evaluated. The synthesis distills recurring design patterns and failure modes into explicit
insights, and identifies open directions, including long-horizon consistency, physical and causal grounding,
measurable synergy between understanding and generation, and standardized evaluation of world properties.
Open resources. A public reading list, taxonomy, codes, data, and resources are all categorized and
available at project page.

The remainder of this survey proceeds as follows. Section 2 formalizes the video–world relation, the two
forms of consistency, and the four operations. Section 3 assembles the shared background: recurring video
scenarios, language-model and generative-modeling foundations, and the definition and taxonomy of video
world models. Sections 4 through 7 then follow the operations from observing a trace to evolving a world state.
Section 4 studies how models read semantic, spatial, temporal, physical, and causal properties from video;
Section 5 examines the representations, generative paradigms, and controls used to write traces; Section 6
turns to the architectures and training regimes that connect the two directions; and Section 7 completes the
progression with open-loop and online models that evolve video-derived state under actions or conditions.
Figure 2 condenses the resulting landscape: each operation branches into parallel research streams and then
into the representative works examined in those chapters. Section 8 distills the challenges that cut across the
four operations into directions for future work, and a brief conclusion closes the survey.
2
The World-Modeling Lens
This section defines the world and its video (Section 2.1), two forms of consistency used later for evaluation
(Section 2.2), and the four operations that organize Sections 4–7 (Section 2.3). Table 1 lists the notation.
9


Video Foundation Models
World-Modeling Lens
2.1 World, Video, and Represented State
We distinguish three objects: the evolving world, the video through which it is observed, and the representations
that a model constructs from available information. The following definitions provide a conceptual abstraction:
Definition 2.1 (Latent temporal world) The latent temporal world is a stochastic process W = (wt)t≥1
over a state space W. Each state wt ∈W encompasses entities, their attributes and relations, and
relevant physical and dynamic variables. Assuming that wt contains sufficient information for predicting
subsequent evolution, its dynamics can be expressed as
wt+1 ∼T ( · | wt, at),
where at denotes an optional intervention or control input, with uncontrolled evolution recovered when
no such input is supplied.
Definition 2.2 (Observed video) An observed video V = (x1, . . . , xT ) is a temporally ordered sequence
of visual observations generated by a sensing process, xt ∼O( · | wt, πt), where πt is the camera
configuration. We refer to an observed or generated video V as a trace. This trace is partial: xt depends
only on the part of wt visible under πt, which changes with camera motion, shot changes, or multiple
cameras; lossy: sensing, resolution, compression, and editing remove information, so distinct world
states may produce indistinguishable observations; and temporally ordered: frame order and duration
carry evidence about motion and state transition, and thus about T .
Definition 2.3 (Represented state) A model with parameters θ may construct an internal representation
st ∼qθ(· | x≤t, a<t), inferred from the observations and actions available up to time t; a deterministic
encoder st = fθ(x≤t, a<t) is the special case of a point mass, and a transition model may instead
sample st+1 from its own predictions (Definition 2.6). In general, st̸ = wt, and its information about
the underlying world may be incomplete or inaccurate.
Remark. Definitions 2.1–2.3 describe a partially observable dynamical system, closely related to the envi-
ronment model underlying a partially observable Markov decision process. Such formulations are widely
used in model-based reinforcement learning [143, 146], where agents infer predictive states from observations
and simulate possible futures under candidate actions. For general video foundation models, however, the
represented state may be implicit or constructed from an entire sequence rather than updated online.
2.2 Two Forms of Consistency
Because video observes the world only partially (Definition 2.2), a trace can be consistent at two levels.
Definition 2.4 (Temporal coherence and world consistency) Let ˆV = (ˆx1, . . . , ˆxT ) be a trace that a model
generates or predicts. ˆV is temporally coherent if neighboring frames agree locally, d(ˆxt, ˆxt+1) ≤δ for
all t, where d penalizes implausible local motion, appearance change, or flicker. ˆV is world-consistent
relative to specified dynamics and constraints, if its observations are compatible with at least one
admissible world trajectory:
∃w1:T ∈C, π1:T
s.t.
ˆxt ∈supp O(· | wt, πt),
∀t,
where C contains the trajectories that satisfy the assumed dynamics, identity persistence, spatial
relations, and relevant physical and causal constraints under the specified interventions. When the
camera path is specified as a condition, π1:T is fixed to it instead of quantified.
10


Video Foundation Models
World-Modeling Lens
Observation 2.5 (Coherence-consistency) Neither property implies the other. An object that changes identity
after leaving the frame, a room that alters its layout between visits, or an action whose effect later
disappears can yield locally smooth frames that no trajectory in C explains; conversely, a hard cut violates
local smoothness while remaining world-consistent.
Temporal coherence is a property of the trace; world consistency is a property of the world the trace implies.
A recorded video is world-consistent by construction, because a real trajectory produced it, so the distinction
matters for traces that a model writes or predicts. World-modeling evidence must therefore test world
consistency, not only temporal coherence. As O and C are unobserved, Definition 2.4 is an evaluation target,
not a metric; benchmarks in Sections 5.1.2 and 7.4 probe it via revisits, occlusion, and interventions.
2.3 The Four Operations
Definition 2.6 (Four operations)
Y Reading V →W (video comprehension).
Reading infers selected properties of the world from
observed video, ˆϕt ∼rθ(· | st), an estimate of ϕ(wt), where st is constructed from the available
observations and ϕ ranges over semantic, spatial, temporal, physical, and causal properties. It is
evaluated by accuracy against ϕ(wt) and by calibrated uncertainty for occluded or off-screen content.
Ò Writing W →V (video generation). Writing samples a trace from a condition,
ˆx1:T ∼pθ(x1:T | c), where c (text, an image or video prefix, audio, camera motion, trajectories, or
layouts) specifies the world only in part and the generator resolves the rest. It is evaluated by
perceptual quality, adherence to c, and world consistency of ˆV (Definition 2.4).
T Sharing V ↔W (unified comprehension and generation). Sharing couples a reading map with
parameters θR and a writing map with parameters θW through at least one shared component:
parameters (θR ∩θW̸ = ∅), tokens or representations, an interface, or a training objective. It is
evaluated at three levels: capability coverage, architectural sharing, and functional synergy, that is,
measurable improvement of reading, writing, or both under matched resources.
 Interacting W ⟲(video world modeling). Interacting rolls out a transition model,
st+1 ∼pθ(st+1 | s≤t, at, ct), with ˆxt+1 = gθ(st+1) when visible output is needed. The state st may be
frames, video tokens, or latent features, and the actions at and conditions ct are fixed before an
open-loop rollout or supplied online. It is evaluated by whether predicted changes follow these inputs
and stay world-consistent (Definition 2.4), beyond the quality of rendered frames.
The arrows here are conceptual shorthand: writing reaches W only through c, and reading and writing are
not exact inverses. Stronger claims about the represented state need stronger evidence; Table 2 gives four
levels for each operation, from plausible output to decision utility. One system can perform several operations:
a unified editor reads a source clip before writing its revision, and an interactive world model reads its history,
writes each next frame, and updates its state in one backbone. Figure 1 places milestones of the four lines of
work on one timeline, from video encoders [8, 102] and generators [162, 169] to unified models [479, 531] and
action-conditioned simulators [32, 172].
3
Preliminaries
This section covers the background shared by the four operations: video scenarios (Section 3.1), video large
language models (Section 3.2), generative modeling (Section 3.3), and the scope and taxonomy of video world
models (Section 3.4).
3.1 Video Scenarios
Video modeling spans diverse scenarios that differ in temporal extent, observation perspective, modality
composition, information availability, interaction, and domain-specific constraints. Rather than forming
mutually exclusive categories, these characteristics impose complementary demands on what a model must

observe, retain, predict, and control. Table 3 lists the evidence, modeling demands, and representative

Three labels describe the recorded content: its default form, its modalities, and its viewpoint. Regular short
video, the standard setting for representation learning and clip-level understanding, consists of passive third-
person clips of seconds to minutes, whose audio is usually ignored. Its datasets annotate action classes [208],
actions defined by temporal change [132], or captions [541], but do not test memory, online response, or control.
Sounding video treats synchronized audio as evidence, with tasks that range from audio-event recognition and
source localization to generating events whose sound and appearance must agree [50, 124, 449]. Its benchmarks
must rule out visual or language shortcuts. Egocentric video is recorded from a wearer’s or agent’s viewpoint,
so hands, manipulated objects, gaze, and body-coupled camera motion enter the stream [78, 133, 134, 336].
The task shifts from recognizing actions to tracking object state and intent across long activities with frequent
occlusion.
Three further labels describe how the model accesses observations and whether it can change them. In
streaming and online video, frames arrive incrementally, so a model must update its state and respond within
bounded latency, without access to future frames. Typical tasks are online question answering and streaming
captioning, which may require timestamped or proactive responses [352, 569]. The same recording can be
processed offline in full or presented as a stream. Long-form video spans minutes to hours: the full recording
is usually available, but not every frame can be processed at full resolution, and the evidence for a query is
sparse and spread across distant segments [109, 472, 509, 654]. A model must therefore decide which evidence
to keep and link events that lie far apart in time. Interactive video lets a human, policy, or agent supply
actions that change what is observed next [7, 406, 575]. Unlike in a passive stream, the future then depends
on the interaction history; interactive world models (Section 3.4) target this setting.
Domain-specific video cuts across all of these labels: driving [33, 416], surgery [456], sports [127], and
instructional video [343, 437] impose their own event structure, terminology, and costs of error. These domains
test whether general video representations transfer to rare events and specialized outputs.
3.2 Video Large Language Models
Connecting video to an LLM. A Video-LLM feeds video into a pretrained large language model (LLM),
usually an autoregressive Transformer [454, 460] trained by next-token prediction (Equation (2)). The LLM

learns to follow instructions through supervised instruction tuning [500], which visual instruction tuning
extends to images [282], and is further aligned with human or model feedback [361] and verifiable rewards [138,
228]. Inside a video system, it understands and generates language, answers questions, and sometimes acts as
a controller that decomposes tasks, selects tools, and reasons over several steps. In a common division of labor,
a video encoder supplies perceptual features, the LLM interprets or organizes them, and a decoder, generator,
or world model produces pixels or state transitions. To connect video, a video encoder Ev maps frames or
clips x1:T to visual features, and a connector P converts these features into a language-compatible sequence:
z1:L = P(Ev(x1:T )) .
(1)
Connectors range from linear projectors [282] to learned-query resamplers [196, 246] and gated cross-
attention [2]; Section 4.3 compares them and the video-specific training recipes.
Video tokens and context. In the Video-LLM literature, a video token usually denotes one of the continuous
embeddings in z1:L passed to the LLM. Autoregressive video generators often use the same term for discrete
codebook indices, a different representation (Section 5). Frame sampling, temporal pooling, and the connector
set the number L of embeddings kept from a video. A larger L preserves finer spatial and temporal evidence
but costs more context, memory, and attention; stronger compression is cheaper but can remove brief events,
small objects, or long-range relations. Because the context window is fixed and also holds the instructions,
earlier dialogue, and the output, long-video models must split the remaining budget between temporal coverage
and visual detail.
3.3 Generative Modeling Foundations
Conditional generation. Whereas the LLM mainly produces language, generative models produce the visual
output of a video system. Writing samples a trace ˆx1:T ∼pθ(x1:T | c) (Definition 2.6). Video generators
usually work in the latent space of a variational autoencoder (VAE) [214], whose decoder may be trained
with an adversarial loss [130]: an encoder maps the video to a latent z, the generator models pθ(z | c), and
13


Video Foundation Models
World-Modeling Lens
the decoder maps a sampled latent back to frames. We write z for whichever target is modeled, whether
continuous latents, discrete video tokens, or, for pixel-space models, the frames themselves. In general, a
condition may specify content, such as a prompt or a reference image, or control how the state evolves, such
as actions, trajectories, or camera paths. The same modality can play different roles: an image may specify
appearance, provide the initial observation, or serve as a spatial reference. Action-conditioned and interactive
world models (Section 3.4) require the second kind.
Autoregressive and masked modeling. When the target is a sequence of ordered units z1:N, an autoregressive
model factorizes the conditional distribution as
pθ(z1:N | c) =

i=1
pθ(zi | z<i, c)
(2)
and generates the units in order, each sampled unit joining the context for later predictions. The units may
be discrete codes or continuous latent blocks, ordered by time alone or by a space–time serialization, and
Transformer decoders are the common implementation [460]. Masked modeling instead hides a subset of units
and predicts them in parallel from the visible context and the condition. For discrete targets, with sampled
masked positions M and visible units z ¯

MaskGIT [42] turns these predictions into samples through iterative filling and refinement. Continuous pixel
or feature targets instead use a reconstruction loss such as squared error [154].
Diffusion and flow matching. A diffusion model learns to reverse a forward process that gradually corrupts
data with noise [167, 418]. Let (z0, c) ∼pdata be a clean training latent and its condition, and let k ∈{1, . . . , K}
index the denoising step; the subscript k is diffusion time, distinct from video time t. In the noise-prediction
form, z0 is corrupted to zk = √¯αk z0 + √1 −¯αk ϵ, where ϵ ∼N(0, I) and ¯αk decreases toward 0 as k increases,
and the network learns to recover the noise:
Ldiff = E(z0,c),ϵ,k

Sampling starts from zK ∼N(0, I) and applies denoising updates zK →· · · →zk →zk−1 →· · · →z0, after

matching instead trains a vector field that transports a simple base distribution to the data along a chosen
probability path [280]. Here k is continuous: for the linear path zk = (1 −k)z0 + kϵ with k ∼U[0, 1], the
target velocity is ϵ −z0:

Sampling integrates dzk/dk = vθ(zk, k, c) from noise at k = 1 to data at k = 0, in practice over a discrete grid
of steps. Diffusion and flow matching can share backbones and samplers. Section 4 uses masked prediction to
learn representations, Section 5 covers generators built on these objectives, and Section 7 adapts them to
action-conditioned online rollout.
3.4 Taxonomy of Video World Models
A video world model performs the interacting operation of Definition 2.6 with a video-centered state. Unlike
a general video generator, which aims at plausible content, it learns how that state evolves. We exclude
models whose primary persistent state is an explicit 3D structure, such as a neural field, 3D Gaussians, or an
occupancy grid, as well as purely symbolic or text-based world models; models that keep 3D structure only as
auxiliary memory for a video-centered state remain in scope (Section 7.2.2). World models from model-based
reinforcement learning are included only when their state is learned from video.
We classify video world models along two independent axes. On the first axis, the prediction space, observation-
space models predict future frames, video tokens, or generative latents decoded to video, as GAIA [172, 399]
and EVA [71] do. Although VAE latents are not pixels, predicting them for decoding to video still counts as
14


Video Foundation Models
World-Modeling Lens
observation-space prediction. Latent-state models instead predict future video-derived features that need not
be decoded to video: V-JEPA [20] and V-JEPA 2 [9] learn such features by masked feature prediction, which
serves as pretraining for latent transition models (Section 7), and DINO-WM [652] and V-JEPA 2-AC add
action-conditioned transitions for planning. The second axis, the transition interface, describes how controls
reach the model. Passive models predict from observations alone, while action-conditioned models receive
a control sequence fixed before rollout, such as the ego trajectories of Vista [118] or the robot actions of
IRASim [663]. Interactive models accept new controls during rollout, so each input can change what follows,
as in GameNGen [459], Genie 2 [366], and related playable simulators [4, 32, 156, 631]. Section 7 reviews each
cell of the resulting 2 × 3 grid (Figure 10).
4
Y Video Comprehension: Reading the World
Reading means inferring world content, structure, dynamics, physics, and causality from partial video traces.
Video comprehension performs the reading operation V →W of Section 2: given a trace V , the model
estimates properties of the latent world W that produced it. From a limited viewpoint and interval, it
must recover which entities and events are present, where they are, how they change, which constraints
govern that change, and what may have caused it. Occlusion, camera motion, editing, sparse sampling, and
missing modalities leave much of this state unobserved, and the viewing scenarios of Section 3.1 change how
much evidence must be selected and remembered. Sections 4.1–4.5 cover tasks and benchmarks, encoders,
Video-LLMs, agents, and applications in turn. Tang et al. [438] survey Video-LLMs in more detail; here we
organize the evaluation of reading by the world properties it tests (Section 4.1) and review Video-LLMs as
one stage of the reading pipeline.
4.1 Tasks, Data, and Evaluation
We organize video-understanding tasks by the main dimension of world state they require: semantic (enti-
ties, actions, events), spatial (location, identity, layout, viewpoint), temporal (order, duration, boundaries,
long-range dependencies), physical (contact, gravity, containment, material behavior), and causal (effects,
interventions, counterfactuals). The dimensions interact, as when tracking combines identity with temporal
continuity, so Table 4 places each benchmark under the dimension it mainly tests, even when the text
introduces it elsewhere. Output format is a separate axis: a label, span, mask, or sentence can probe any
dimension, and what a benchmark measures depends on the evidence needed to produce its output.
Aggregate accuracy does not show that a model used the intended evidence, since many questions can be
answered from the text, a single frame, or static appearance. Text-only, single-frame, shuffled-order, and
restricted-context baselines expose these shortcuts, and TempCore [358] and Video-Index [420] use them to
filter existing benchmarks down to vision-dependent, order-sensitive subsets. Minimal pairs of near-identical
videos with different answers block appearance-only solutions (TimeBlind [235], MVP [222]).
4.1.1 Semantic Understanding
Semantic understanding identifies the entities, actions, events, and relations that make a video meaningful.
Action recognition is the most established semantic task: UCF101 [424] and HMDB51 [225] test closed-set
recognition in trimmed clips, Kinetics [208] scales up the number and diversity of classes, and Something-
Something [132] makes labels depend on how objects change under manipulation. Language moves the output
beyond fixed categories through retrieval (MSR-VTT [541]), captioning (VATEX [478], YouCook2 [655]), and
video question answering (VideoQA: ActivityNet-QA [594], TVQA [234], TGIF-QA [199]).
Recent Video-LLM benchmarks mix semantic questions with temporal, spatial, and knowledge-intensive
items (Video-MME and Video-MME-v2 [109, 110], MMVU [643], Video-MMMU [177, 319]). Hallucination
benchmarks test whether the reported content is supported by the video at all: VideoHallucer [322, 488]
uses adversarially paired questions, EventHallusion [613] probes events suggested by language priors, and
ARGUS [391] scores hallucination and omission in free-form captions.

4.1.2 Spatial Understanding
Spatial understanding asks where entities are, how their extent changes, and whether their identity persists as
viewpoint and visibility vary. Spatiotemporal grounding links a referring expression to a region tube over an
interval (VidSTG [635]). Video object segmentation tracks pixel masks (DAVIS [373], YouTube-VOS [543]),
referring variants select the target by language or motion (Ref-YouTube-VOS [404], MeViS [83]; addressed by
ReferFormer [514]), and MOTChallenge [344] and LaSOT [94] test identity across occlusion and reappearance.
Memory-based and promptable trackers such as XMem [67], AOT [571], DEVA [66], and SAM 2 [390] keep
earlier evidence as the target changes appearance or disappears.
Spatial reasoning benchmarks go beyond masks and tracks: VSI-Bench [559] and MMSI-Video-Bench [274]
test relative position, viewpoint, and layout, STI-Bench [262] asks for quantitative pose and motion, and
DSI-Bench [636] separates observer motion from object motion. A newer group tests state that is out of view:
SCP-Bench [642] infers unseen past or future layouts, Beyond3D [327] asks where relocated objects now are,
and OVO-S-Bench [260] answers from the video prefix seen so far. These tasks test the object permanence
and revisit consistency that world models must maintain (Section 7.2.2).
16


Video Foundation Models
World-Modeling Lens
4.1.3 Temporal Understanding
Temporal understanding recovers when an event occurs, how long it lasts, and how evidence at different
moments is ordered or connected. Temporal action detection locates predefined actions in untrimmed video
(THUMOS [195], ActivityNet [160]), and moment retrieval returns the segment that matches a language
query (Charades-STA [115], QVHighlights [233], DiDeMo [161]).
Dense event captioning localizes and
describes possibly overlapping events (ActivityNet Captions [221], addressed end to end by Vid2Seq [555]),
and anticipation predicts an action not yet observed (e.g., Ego4D forecasting [133]).
Fine-grained and long-form benchmarks stress subtle temporal differences and sparse evidence. Temporal-
Bench [35] and TempCompass [303] test ordering, speed, and direction, TimeBlind [235] pairs videos that differ
only in temporal structure, and Video-MME-Logical [227] separates logical complexity from scene recognition.
MLVU [654], LVBench [472], and LongVideoBench [509] pose heterogeneous questions over long videos, and
VideoOdyssey [151] extends this to ultra-long, omni-modal video, measuring how much context stays usable
as evidence length grows.
Streaming benchmarks make state maintenance directly observable. SVCBench [291] asks for running object
and event counts at many points in a video, TRACE [329] scores answer quality together with latency, false
alarms, and missed responses under causal input, and StreamingEval [436] adds encoding throughput, time to
first token, and a fixed memory budget.
4.1.4 Physical Understanding
Physical understanding concerns how a visible state can change under constraints that are often only
partly observed. Plausibility benchmarks ask whether an observed transition is possible: IntPhys [395]
and IntPhys 2 [30] test object permanence and intuitive physics, ImplausiBench [345] contains videos that
violate expected physical behavior, and Physion [23] instead asks whether objects will make contact. Other
benchmarks ask for next states, interaction outcomes, or the mechanism behind a change. CLEVRER [578] asks
descriptive, predictive, and explanatory questions about collisions, while PhysicsMind [335], PhysBench [72],
and PAI-Bench-U [651] broaden coverage to object properties, commonsense constraints, task completion, and
next actions. APT [515] further labels timestamped transitions by mechanism (contact, gravity, or friction).
Two benchmarks check that answers rest on physical evidence rather than priors. MVP [222] pairs near-
identical videos that require opposite answers, and VisPhyWorld [268] asks the model to rebuild the scene as
executable simulator code so that its physical hypothesis can be checked. Physics benchmarks for generated
video, such as Physics-IQ [346] and VideoPhy [16, 17], are covered in Sections 5.1.2 and 7.4.
4.1.5 Causal Understanding
Causal understanding asks why an observed event occurred and how the outcome would change if an action,
object, or condition were different. On synthetic collisions, CLEVRER [578] adds counterfactual questions
to its explanatory ones. Real-world and procedural benchmarks link actions to effects that may appear
much later: NExT-QA [526] and STAR [507] ask about event relations and reasons, Video-Holmes [68] and
Causal2Needles [250] spread the clues far apart, and CaST-Bench [621] covers more varied settings and causal
structures. Counterfactual benchmarks test altered outcomes directly: CausalVQA [108] asks counterfactual,
hypothetical, and planning questions about real egocentric video, and DMC-CF [618] pairs such questions
about real videos with causal-graph interventions, whose regenerated variants also limit data contamination.
4.2 Video Representation Learning and Encoding
Video representation learning maps frames to features that can be reused across recognition, retrieval,
grounding, reasoning, and multimodal modeling. Convolutional backbones such as I3D [39] and SlowFast [102]
established supervised spatiotemporal processing, and TimeSformer [25], ViViT [8], and Video Swin [304]
carried the Vision Transformer [86] to video at different spatial and temporal scales. The pretraining objective
decides what these backbones retain, and we group objectives by their dominant supervision signal into the
four families of Figure 3. The families overlap: masked feature prediction, for instance, is both masked and
predictive. Variational autoencoders and tokenizers built for synthesis are covered in Section 5.

Figure 3 Four families of video representation learning. They differ in how supervision targets are constructed and

4.2.1 Contrastive Learning
Contrastive learning pulls positive pairs together in embedding space and pushes mismatched pairs apart,
commonly with an InfoNCE-style objective. In video-only learning, CVRL [380] and ρMoCo [103] draw
positives from augmentations or clips of the same video, and the sampling distance sets a trade-off: views too
close give little contrast, while views too far apart may show different events.
Video–text contrastive learning pairs clips with language. Among dual-encoder retrieval models, Frozen in
Time [14] trains a space–time Transformer jointly on image–text and video–text pairs, and CLIP4Clip [316]
transfers the CLIP image–text encoder and adds temporal aggregation, while VideoCLIP [538], X-CLIP [330],
ViCLIP [481], and Finsta [101] develop video-specific alignment. Foundation encoders combine contrastive
pretraining with masked or generative losses (InternVideo [483], InternVideo2 [482]), distillation into a masked-
video student (VideoPrism [637]), or later alignment stages for language and dense prediction (Perception
Encoder [29]). Global alignment can drop fine-grained detail: the World Embedding Benchmark [301] shows
that alignment and recoverable physical state can diverge, as physics-specific contrastive tuning improves
video–text matching but weakens physical-quantity regression from frozen features; the added losses aim to
retain it.
4.2.2 Masked Learning
Masked learning removes much of the spatiotemporal input and trains the model to recover it, an idea that
VideoBERT [426] applied early to quantized video tokens and speech transcripts jointly. VideoMAE [453]
reconstructs pixels behind tube masks, with a high mask ratio that exploits the redundancy of adjacent frames,
and VideoMAE-V2 [469] scales it with dual masking. Feature targets replace raw pixels: MaskFeat [497]
reconstructs handcrafted or learned features, and UMT [249] distills from an unmasked teacher that sees the
full clip. Learned or teacher targets shift capacity from low-level appearance toward the semantics and motion
that the teacher encodes, but they also inherit its biases.
18


Video Foundation Models
World-Modeling Lens
4.2.3 Predictive Learning
Predictive learning trains a model to predict the latent representation of hidden or future content, without
pixel reconstruction or negative examples. V-JEPA [20] predicts the features of masked regions against a
slowly updated target encoder, V-JEPA 2 [9] scales this to larger and more diverse data, and V-JEPA 2.1 [348]
improves dense features. InternVideo-Next [464] bridges the masked and predictive families, combining
masked reconstruction with latent prediction of its first-stage targets, without video–text data. Adding an
action-conditioned transition model to such a representation yields the action-conditioned latent-state world
models of Section 7.
4.2.4 Generative Learning
Generative pretraining shapes a representation by requiring it to produce missing or future content. Visual
autoregressive objectives [553] predict future frames, patches, or tokens, which rewards sensitivity to appearance
and motion but can spend capacity on reconstruction detail that recognition does not need. Vision-language
generation outputs text instead: GIT-style [467] training generates captions or answers from visual features,
and VideoCoCa [551] combines captioning with contrastive alignment. In both cases, the representation is
shaped only by what captions describe. Large video diffusion models offer a third route: Gen4U [213] reuses a
frozen generator’s intermediate activations for classification, geometry, and captioning, and GenCeption [468]
adapts a diffusion backbone into a text-steered model for depth, normals, pose, segmentation, and keypoints
(Section 6.2).
4.3 Video-Language Foundation Modeling
Video-LLMs extend language foundation models to video for open-ended description, dialogue, question
answering, grounding, and reasoning. They take visual features from encoders such as those in Section 4.2
and must decide which frames to observe, how to encode time, and how many visual tokens the language
model can afford. We review their architectures, training, and test-time scaling in turn.
4.3.1 Architectures
Visual front end. Many systems apply an image encoder such as CLIP [387] or SigLIP [455, 605] to sampled
frames, a route that is cheap and semantically strong but leaves temporal modeling to later modules. Native-
video encoders [9, 662] model local motion before fusion, and direct patch or visual-token inputs keep finer
spatial detail at a higher context cost. No encoder can recover an event from an unsampled frame.
Time and position. Frame order may be encoded by temporal embeddings, timestamps, or multidimensional
rotary positions. Qwen2.5-VL [13] uses M-RoPE to coordinate temporal and spatial indices, Qwen3-VL [12]
adds textual timestamps so that answers can cite moments, and the InternVL series [474] advances visual
positions in fractional steps so that long sequences stay within the trained positional range. Temporal
Transformers and state-space models such as Mamba [135] can also aggregate frame sequences before they
reach the LLM.
Vision-language connector. The connector maps visual features into the language backbone’s embedding space
and token budget. Linear or MLP projectors turn each visual feature into a prefix token, as in LLaVA-style
systems [282], while Q-Former and resampler designs [246] extract a fixed number of query tokens, as in early
Video-LLaMA [610] and VideoChat [247]. Gated cross-attention, as in Flamingo [2], instead lets language
layers consult visual features at selected depths. Structure-preserving compressors downsample while keeping
spatial layout, through convolutional abstractors (Honeybee [40]), spatial-temporal convolution (VideoLLaMA
2 [70]), or pixel unshuffle (InternVL [474]). Projectors keep the most direct token path, resamplers and
cross-attention give the most control over how much visual information enters the LLM, and compressors
trade some directness for locality-aware reduction.
Compression and memory. Video token count grows with both the number of frames and the tokens per
frame, and pooling, merging, or resampling reduce it at the risk of erasing small objects, short events, or
precise timestamps. Some designs spend tokens unevenly over time: LLaVA-Video [632] keeps a few frames
dense and pools the rest, Keye-VL-1.5 [556] routes frames with large visual change to a high-resolution

as few as two tokens, and Video-LaVIT [206] pairs keyframes with compact motion vectors. For long video,
LongVA [622] and LongVILA [64] rely on long-context transfer and token budgeting, and Video-XL [415] adds
structured compression. One finding bounds this design space: a few tokens carry most of the discriminative
information, but finding them without an oracle is hard [149]. Memory solves a different problem from
compression: MovieChat [419] and MA-LMM [150] maintain a memory of past segments, which decides what
remains available after the original frames leave the context (agent memory is treated in Section 4.4).
Language backbone and output. An instruction-following LLM combines the retained evidence with the
query and produces dialogue, descriptions, explanations, or structured predictions. Early systems extended
image-language models to video with adapters and compressed frame tokens [247, 258, 271, 332, 610], and
later open models add larger multimodal mixtures, more efficient video tokens, and grounded or reasoning
outputs [12, 13, 236, 256, 474, 554, 573, 606]. Boxes, timestamps, and masks can be emitted as special tokens
or by attached heads, which makes the evidence behind an answer easier to inspect. Closed systems such as
Gemini [440] show strong long-context video and audio results but disclose few design details. Table 5 lists
the encoder, connector, and LLM of representative open systems and what each training stage updates.
Streaming and audio-visual input. Streaming models must answer from the evidence seen so far and bound
their state with windows, recurrent memory, or key–value (KV) caches. VideoLLM-online [52] introduced
real-time dialogue over a continuously advancing stream, StreamingVLM [544] keeps a compact KV cache
for unbounded streams, and OneStreamer [603] writes timestamped caption memory and learns when to
record and when to respond. For audio-video input, Qwen2.5-Omni [539] and Qwen3-Omni [540] place both
modalities on one timeline with time-aligned multimodal rotary positions.

4.3.2 Learning Paradigms
Video-LLMs are usually trained in stages because the encoder, connector, and language backbone start from
different representations. In alignment pretraining, the encoder and LLM are often frozen while the connector
learns from caption pairs, video–text matching, and grounding data, following the visual instruction tuning
recipe for images [282]. Table 6 lists pair corpora and instruction datasets for this stage and the next.
Supervised instruction tuning. Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) then trains on VideoQA and dialogue data
for question-conditioned answers, temporal grounding data for timestamps [379], and spatial grounding
21


Video Foundation Models
World-Modeling Lens
data for coordinates or other structured outputs. Which components are updated varies: early Video-
ChatGPT [332] tunes only its projector, Video-LLaVA [271] also updates the LLM, and VideoLLaMA3 [606]
and VideoChat3 [256] adapt both the visual and language components (Table 5). The mixture of image,
short-video, long-video, optical character recognition (OCR), and grounding data decides whether a model
mainly describes clips or can locate and reason over the evidence behind an answer.
Preference optimization and reinforcement learning. Post-training can then optimize preferences or task
outcomes. Reinforcement learning (RL) from human feedback [361] and direct preference optimization, which
Qwen2.5-VL [13] and VistaDPO [183] apply after SFT, improve visual faithfulness only insofar as the feedback
separates grounded content from fluent but unsupported answers. Reasoning-oriented methods instead use
verifiable rewards, often with group relative policy optimization (GRPO) [138]. Video-R1 [105] adds a temporal
reward that compares rollouts on ordered and shuffled frames, LongVILA-R1 [63] scales RL to long videos
through its data and distributed training, and OneThinker [106] broadens the task mixture and normalizes
advantages with running per-task reward statistics.
Task-specific rewards target the output directly: Time-R1 [480] rewards timestamp intersection-over-union for
temporal grounding, VideoChat-R1 [255] jointly rewards spatiotemporal tasks without hurting general QA, and
VideoP2R [202] rewards perception and reasoning separately. Others modify GRPO itself: DeepVideo-R1 [365]
recasts it as advantage regression with difficulty-aware augmentation, and GRPO-CARE [62] adds a bonus if
a slowly updated reference model reproduces the answer from the reasoning trace.
Data and scaling. Data and curriculum matter as much as model size. Current recipes such as Intern-
Video3 [554], Qwen3-VL [12], and VideoLLaMA3 [606] train jointly on image, video, and language data and
widen task coverage with synthetic instructions; InternVideo3 and Qwen3-VL also add long-context curricula
and use on-policy distillation to supply responses from states the model actually visits. Apollo [667] studies
this design space systematically, measuring how frame sampling, encoder choice, and token allocation interact
across model scales. Newer corpora add finer temporal supervision: OneStreamer-1M [603] times its captions
and QA to the evidence available at each response point.
4.3.3 Test-Time Scaling
Test-time scaling spends extra computation after training to read the available video better. Multimodal chain-
of-thought [100, 318] spends tokens on intermediate reasoning, which helps only if those steps stay grounded
in the frames. Query-aware frame selection instead spends compute on choosing the input: SeViLA [591] and
VideoTree [494] rank, retain, or hierarchically organize frames for the query. Iterative “thinking with video”
methods such as FrameThinker [159] and VideoChat-A1 [493] alternate between reasoning and revisiting frames
over several steps. Search and aggregation select among several reasoning paths by self-consistency, best-of-N,
or verifier and process-reward models, while V-Reason [425] directly modifies the model’s inference-time
behavior instead of sampling more outputs. Each route trades latency and repeated visual computation for
broader exploration or a second look at ambiguous evidence.
The boundary with agentic modeling is gradual: thinking-with-video methods already revisit frames, and
once a model issues explicit operations to fetch new crops, frames, retrieval results, or persistent memory, it
enters the agentic setting of Section 4.4.
4.4 Agentic Video Modeling
Agentic video modeling turns evidence acquisition into a sequential decision problem: an LLM or Video-LLM
forms subquestions, chooses a temporal region or visual detail to inspect, invokes an operation, and updates its
reasoning from the result until it can answer. Its actions change access to the recording by locating segments,
sampling frames, cropping regions, querying tracks, or retrieving memory; actions that change the depicted
world belong to the interactive rollouts of Section 7.
4.4.1 Video Agents
Early video agents separate the controller’s reasoning state from the full video stream (Table 7). Two systems
named VideoAgent take different routes: VideoAgent (long-form) [477] has an LLM iteratively gather a small

set of question-relevant frames, and VideoAgent (memory) [95] builds event and object-tracking memory
and exposes segment localization and memory queries as tools. VideoAgent2 [649] adds uncertainty-aware
replanning, so that new observations can revise the search and intermediate evidence is verified. Later
controllers refine the observation policy: VCA [567] explores unexamined segments out of curiosity, and
VideoMind [300] splits the loop into planner, grounder, verifier, and answerer roles, all chain-of-LoRA experts
on one backbone.
Agentic search extends adaptive observation from frame selection to a structured video collection. Deep Video
Discovery [629] segments long video into a multi-granular database and lets the controller choose search
tools and parameters, and VideoExplorer [596] poses sub-questions inside the reasoning loop and locates the
moments that answer them.
Continuous streams add another decision: when to observe and when to speak, possibly before the user asks.
StreamAgent [558] anticipates task-relevant events and shifts attention before they arrive, and AViLA [608]
answers asynchronous queries once the accumulated evidence is sufficient.
Memory determines what an agent retains between observations. Flat caption lists are cheap to query
but lose temporal scope and visual detail. VideoLucy [669] instead backtracks through a coarse-to-fine
memory hierarchy and MM-Mem [266] builds a sensory, episodic, and schema-level memory pyramid, while
InternVideo3 [554] accumulates observations, reasoning, and tool actions in one evolving context. Memory-
centric agents aim for competence that grows with history instead of resetting at every query: M3-Agent [308]

consolidates an entity-centric long-term memory from continuous audio-visual input, and WorldMM [577]
maintains a dynamic multimodal memory.
Multi-agent collaboration divides the loop across specialized roles. VideoMultiAgents [226] coordinates vision,
text, and graph specialists, LongVideoAgent [292] pairs a reasoning master with grounding assistants, and
Think, Then Verify [490] makes candidate answers survive adversarial checking. MAPA [547] pairs lightweight
perception agents with action agents to avoid redundant computation.
Other agents are trained, not only prompted: SAGE [197] learns with RL how far ahead to look, EVA [630]
makes end-to-end RL of a video agent efficient, and CASE [88] learns when to stop searching. Trained policies
can lower tool cost and improve stopping, but they tie the agent to the tools seen in training.
Agent quality depends jointly on the backbone, observation policy, tool outputs, and memory: a wrong
first hypothesis can steer later calls to the wrong interval, and noisy captions or detections propagate
through the reasoning trace. Agents are trained on tool-use traces for SFT and questions for RL (Ego-R1
Data [448], LongVT’s VideoSIAH [572], VideoThinker’s synthetic trajectories [239]). Benchmarks (Table 8)
test sparse-evidence search (VideoSIAH-Eval [572]), multi-hop retrieval with evidence-necessity checks and
tool-call cost (LongVidSearch [590]), trajectories against evidence milestones (AgentVidBench [5]), and
combined video and web search (VideoGAIA [607]). Others test proactive and spoken audio-visual interaction
(IPIBench [245], OmniInteract [312]) and cross-session memory with cases where the needed history is missing
(APM-Bench [184]). Beyond answer accuracy, evaluation should report whether the retrieved evidence supports
the answer and what the search costs; REPAIR [3] adds repeated paired runs to separate run-to-run variance
from method effects and counts the operations and latency hidden behind visible tool calls.
4.4.2 Tool Use and Retrieval Augmentation
Tools supply observations that the base model cannot obtain reliably from its current context: detectors
and trackers for entity state, temporal grounders for event boundaries, croppers for spatial detail, and OCR,
speech recognition, or captioners for other modalities. In modular agents, a prompted controller composes
external tools: VisProg [142] and ViperGPT [431] generate executable programs over vision tools without
training. Native tool-calling models instead learn when to call a tool, with which parameters, and how to use
the result. LongVT [572] surveys a long video, predicts a relevant interval, and crops and densely resamples it
before continuing to reason, and VideoThinker [239] learns temporal retrieval and zoom from tool trajectories
synthesized in caption space and grounded back to video. Ego-R1 [448] trains a chain of tool-thought with
RL over day- and week-long egocentric recordings. Tool use can also be distilled away: Agent-of-Thoughts
24


Video Foundation Models
World-Modeling Lens
Distillation [414] distills agent traces into a single Video-LLM as chain-of-thought supervision, trading flexibility
for one-pass inference.
Retrieval augmentation moves evidence into an external index. One VideoRAG [200] retrieves query-relevant
videos and keeps both their visual and textual content, while another [394] combines graph-based text
grounding with multimodal encoding to search very large collections. The retrieval unit shapes what can be
recovered: fixed-length chunks may split an event, so SceneRAG [602] indexes narrative scenes and adds an
entity-relation graph for multi-hop retrieval, at the cost of errors in segmentation and graph construction.
Tools and retrieval address different bottlenecks: a cropper or grounder fetches finer local evidence on demand,
whereas an index narrows the search across long videos or corpora. Both still require the system to decide
what to request, detect missing or conflicting evidence, and trace the answer back to its source clips.
4.5 Applications
Applications pose these problems under domain-specific viewpoints, vocabularies, and event structures.
4.5.1 Embodied and Egocentric Perception
Egocentric video records activity from the wearer’s viewpoint, so the camera moves with the body, hands
occlude manipulated objects, and key events are separated by long stretches of routine activity. Standard
resources include EPIC-KITCHENS-100 [78], Ego4D [133], EgoSchema [336], and the paired first- and
third-person views of Ego-Exo4D [134] (Section 3.1). An egocentric assistant has to track the manipulated
object and its state, the current task step, and the earlier event that explains the present situation, even when
the object is hidden behind the hand or out of view.
Newer benchmarks target specific settings. EgoTools [447] tests understanding of physical tools and their effects
(not software-tool calls), covering affordance, grounding, procedure, and spatial reasoning, and EgoPolice [129]
tests police body-worn camera footage, where consequential events are rare and visibility is poor.
4.5.2 Autonomous Driving
Driving video must be read as a dynamic scene of road geometry, traffic controls, vehicles, pedestrians, and
their relations. BDD100K [584] supplies diverse annotated road video, and nuScenes [33] and the Waymo
Open Dataset [428] add synchronized camera and range-sensor sequences with 3D annotations. Language-
based benchmarks ask which participants and relations matter: DriveLM [416] links key objects and their
interactions to prediction and planning questions through graph-structured VideoQA, and the frame-level
NuScenes-SpatialQA [445] tests spatial relations in driving scenes. OmniDrive [471] aligns language reasoning
with 3D driving tasks and adds counterfactual questions about how alternative actions would change an
outcome. The resulting scene state links video reading to the trajectory prediction, action selection, and
closed-loop control of Section 7.
4.5.3 Domain-Specific Video Analysis
Medical and surgical video.
Surgical video is structured by procedure phases, so recognizing visible
instruments is not enough. EndoNet [456] introduced Cholec80 for phase and tool recognition, CholecT50
and Rendezvous [356] refine the output into instrument–verb–target triplets, and SurgVLM [604] combines
perception, temporal analysis, and question answering across surgical tasks. Evaluation must check phase,
anatomy, and action against the structured labels of clinical workflows, not the fluency of a description.
Sports analytics. A match contains long stretches of routine play broken by brief, rule-dependent events.
SoccerNet [127] defined action spotting in broadcast soccer; SoccerNet-v2 [80] adds camera-shot segmentation
and replay grounding, which require precise localization and knowledge of broadcast structure.
Film and edited video. In edited footage, shot size, cut type, and transitions are deliberate choices. VEU-
Bench [237] tests recognition, reasoning, and judging of editing attributes within and across shots, and finds
that general Video-LLMs handle them poorly, in some cases below chance.
25


Video Foundation Models
World-Modeling Lens
Education and instructional video. Instructional video follows a procedure from an initial state through
intermediate steps to a goal. HowTo100M [343] supplies narrated demonstrations, and CrossTask [666] and
COIN [437] annotate steps for temporal localization. Reading such video means tracking the progress of the
procedure: separating narrated intention from visible operation, recovering omitted or repeated steps, and
reasoning about order and prerequisites.
­ INSIGHT Video understanding is limited by state, not by context length
Encoders compress observations, Video-LLMs map evidence into language, and agents choose what to inspect,
but all of them are limited by what state they keep and ground, not by how many frames they accept. Longer
context helps only when it corrects or stabilizes that state, so evaluation should ask whether added evidence
improves the state estimate, stays traceable to source clips, and survives later queries.
5
Ò
Video Generation: Writing the World
Writing means rendering a new video trace from a partial world specification supplied as text, images, audio,
motion, or other conditions.
Video generation is the writing operation W →V of Section 2: its conditions fix some entities, relations, and
dynamics of W and leave the rest to the generator. Text may specify an event, an image may fix its initial
appearance, audio may set visible rhythm or articulation, and trajectories camera or object motion.
This chapter covers models whose output and training target are the video itself, judged by fidelity, condition
adherence, temporal coherence, and controllable revision. Persistent state under occlusion, revisits, long
rollouts, and actions is treated in Section 7. We cover tasks and evaluation, latent representations, generative
paradigms, control strategies, and applications. Xing et al. [535] review video diffusion models in depth.
5.1 Tasks, Conditions, and Evaluation
A video-generation task is defined by the information fixed before synthesis: text, images, source videos,
audio, reference identities, or prescribed motion. Evaluation measures the visual and temporal quality of the
result, its adherence to the condition, and the stability of the entities, events, and physical relations it shows.
Table 9 lists training resources, Table 10 lists metrics and learned scorers, and Table 11 lists benchmarks and
the conditions they test.
5.1.1 Generation Tasks by Condition
Text, image, and video conditions. Text-to-video (T2V) maps a description of content, style, or events to a
video and leaves most appearance and motion to the model, as in Sora [31], Wan [462], and HunyuanVideo [219].
Image-to-video (I2V) fixes the initial state or reference appearance and must add plausible motion without
losing that structure and identity, as in Stable Video Diffusion [27] and the human-animation systems
Animate Anyone [179] and MagicAnimate [548]. Video-to-video (V2V) starts from a source video and covers
transformations with different preservation requirements. Editing changes selected content and should stay
local, as in Tune-A-Video [512], Video-P2P [293], FateZero [377], and TokenFlow [126]. Translation maps
the source to another appearance or domain while keeping its content. Interpolation connects observed
endpoints, and extension continues the sequence beyond its last frame, as in NUWA-XL [580], SEINE [60],
and StreamingT2V [162].
Audio and audio-visual conditions. In audio-driven generation, a speech, music, or sound track sets visible
dynamics such as lip motion, expression, gesture, or rhythm, as in Wav2Lip [375], SadTalker [626], EMO [446],
and Hallo [542]. Joint audio-video generation instead produces both streams and must keep their events
synchronized as well as each stream plausible, as in JavisDiT [288], JavisDiT++ [289], and Ovi [309].
Geometric, motion, and identity conditions. Camera, motion, and trajectory conditions prescribe how
the viewpoint or scene elements evolve. Camera poses and paths control the observation process, as in
26


Video Foundation Models
World-Modeling Lens
CameraCtrl [152], while object trajectories, human poses, and action sequences constrain motion within the
scene, as in MotionCtrl [492], DragNUWA [579], VideoComposer [475], and Wan-Move [73]. Identity- or
subject-conditioned generation, as in ConsisID [597], fixes which person, object, or subject persists over time,
and that subject must stay stable under pose, viewpoint, and occlusion changes.
Any-conditioned and unified control. Any-conditioned systems accept a variable subset of text, image, audio,
structure, motion, and reference inputs, as in FullDiT [207], UNIC [576], and Any2Caption [517]. They must
bind each cue to the right entity, region, time span, or modality and reconcile cues of different granularity.
Section 5.4 describes how such conditions enter the model.
Training supervision. Each task needs training pairs that expose its condition–output correspondence (Table 9).
Caption corpora such as Panda-70M [56] and UltraVideo [550] serve T2V, and image conditioning and extension
can be learned by keeping selected frames or prefixes of a clip [203]. Editing needs source–instruction–target
triplets, as in OpenVE-3M [153]. Camera control uses posed video such as RealEstate10K [657], motion
control uses tracked point trajectories [73], and audio-visual generation needs synchronized tracks such
as VGGSound [50]. Identity control pairs references and targets from different contexts or shots, as in
Phantom-Data [65], MuSS [611], and Omni-R2V [253], so the model cannot simply copy the reference’s pose
or background into the output.
5.1.2 Evaluation and Metrics
Video-generation quality has several dimensions, and the right profile depends on the task: T2V emphasizes
semantic alignment, editing requires source preservation and locality, audio-driven generation requires
synchronization, and motion control requires trajectory adherence.
A single aggregate score hides the
failure that limits a system, so we group metrics by the property they measure (Table 10) and benchmarks by
the cases they test (Table 11).
Visual fidelity and temporal quality. FID [165] compares frame-feature distributions and ignores temporal
order. The Inception Score [401] rewards confident, diverse classifier predictions without reference to real
data, which limits its meaning outside the classifier’s domain [21]. FVD [457] extends the comparison to video
features, but it depends on the feature extractor and sample count and can under-penalize temporal corruption
in favor of static samples [121]. JEDi [314] instead uses V-JEPA features and a maximum mean discrepancy,
avoiding FVD’s Gaussian assumption and needing fewer samples. PSNR, SSIM [491], and LPIPS [623] suit
reconstruction and source preservation but penalize valid alternative futures. FVMD [283] and STREAM [212]
target motion consistency and spatiotemporal quality, and VMBench [279] checks whether motion strength
matches the prompt. Diversity needs repeated samples under one condition: DiVid [175] measures variation
in semantics, style, subject, scene, motion, and camera alongside quality and faithfulness.
Condition alignment and compositionality. Condition-aware evaluation checks whether the video follows its
condition. CLIPScore [164] measures image–caption similarity, and its frame-averaged video variants ignore
temporal order and motion. cFreD [220] adds text–visual cross-covariances to a conditional Fréchet distance,
so it penalizes videos swapped among prompts, which a marginal distance cannot detect. FETV [302] and
T2V-CompBench [427] separate objects, attributes, relations, actions, and temporal composition. Structured
conditions have their own tests: MultiRef-Compass [628] checks how several references bind to audio-
visual content. OmniVBench [253] applies case-specific checklists for reference fidelity and binding, and
MoveBench [73] measures trajectory endpoint error. For source-conditioned tasks, V2V-Bench [295] scores
what the instruction should change separately from what the source should preserve.
Physical and world consistency. Realistic local motion can still show impossible interactions, unstable
objects, or broken persistence. VideoPhy [16] and VideoPhy-2 [17] test physical commonsense, the latter in
harder action-centric interactions. PhysicsMind [335] and VideoScience-Bench [178] test mechanics and basic
scientific phenomena, and PhyAVBench [532] tests whether generated sound changes with paired physical
conditions. PhyProbe [223] instead trains a physical-violation scorer on frozen visual features and shows that
prior learned evaluators depend on their annotation distributions, though it retains some dataset dependence
itself. Persistence under revisits and occlusion is tested by the world-model benchmarks of Section 7.4.
Human preference, learned evaluators, and suites. Human comparisons capture coherence, visual appeal,
and instruction adherence that fixed feature distances miss. Learned evaluators model such judgments:

VideoScore [158] predicts several human-rated properties, VideoScore2 [157] adds physical and commonsense
scores with rationales, and MJ-VIDEO [450] trains a reward model on fine-grained preferences. All three
inherit the coverage and biases of their annotations, which for VideoScore2 are partly model-generated. Suites
such as VBench [193] and EvalCrafter [299] combine perceptual, semantic, and temporal dimensions, and
VBench-2.0 [647] extends them to human fidelity, controllability, creativity, physics, and commonsense.
Multi-shot and audio-visual generation. Multi-shot and audio-visual outputs also need evaluation across shot
boundaries and modalities. MSVBench [410] scores story alignment, cross-shot consistency, visual quality,
and motion, while the Cinematic Narrative Benchmark of MuSS [611] separates fidelity to external references
from identity persistence across shots and detects reference copying. JavisBench [288] tests joint generation
of scenes with multiple or overlapping sound events, T2AV-Compass [38] standardizes text-to-audio-video
evaluation, and LongAV-Compass [296] extends it to minute-scale settings where identity, narrative continuity,
and synchronization can fail at different times. At the metric level, Fréchet Audio Distance [211] compares
audio-feature distributions but ignores the video, LSE-D and LSE-C [375] measure speech–mouth alignment,
and JavisScore [288] measures audio-video correspondence in broader sounding scenes.

5.2 Video Latent Representation Learning
Many current video generators work in a compressed latent space: an encoder maps video to a shorter
representation, the generator models its distribution, and a decoder reconstructs the frames. Compression
lowers training and sampling cost, especially for long videos, while the bottleneck and decoder decide
whether fine texture, fast motion, and small objects survive. Figure 4 contrasts the four families: continuous
latents for diffusion and flow objectives, discrete tokens for autoregressive or masked prediction, hybrids that
mix continuous and discrete forms or pair a tokenizer with a diffusion decoder, and semantically aligned
representations that also serve understanding.
5.2.1 Continuous Latents
Per-frame image VAEs. Latent diffusion [396] established the value of training a generative model in the
continuous space of a pretrained image autoencoder, and the simplest video extension encodes each frame
independently. VideoLDM [28] and AnimateDiff [141] reuse image autoencoders and add temporal layers to
image diffusion backbones. This keeps the image prior and eases reuse of text-to-image backbones. However,
the latent sequence still grows linearly with the number of frames, and temporal coherence must be learned
by the generator or by temporal layers in the decoder.
29

Spatiotemporal and Causal VAEs. Video VAEs use 3D or factorized spatiotemporal operations to compress
both resolution and time. Temporal compression shortens the sequence, lowers attention cost, and exploits
redundancy between nearby frames, but an aggressive bottleneck can smooth brief events or fast motion.
CV-VAE [641] keeps its video latent space compatible with a pretrained image VAE so that image-generation
priors remain usable, whereas CogVideoX [570] and Cosmos [355] use dedicated 3D tokenizers for high spatial
and temporal compression. A causal 3D VAE computes the latent at time t without future frames and encodes
the first frame without preceding context, so one tokenizer handles both images and videos and streaming or
autoregressive generators see no future leakage.

5.2.2 Discrete Tokens
From vector quantization to codebook-free schemes. VQ-VAE [359] replaces each encoder output with the
index of a learned codebook entry, producing categorical symbols that next-token or masked-token models
can predict. VQGAN [93] improves high-resolution reconstruction with perceptual and adversarial losses,
and RQ-VAE [231] stacks residual codes to increase precision without enlarging the token grid. Learned
codebooks, however, may be used unevenly or collapse to a small active subset. Later quantizers drop the
learned codebook: FSQ [342] rounds a few scalar dimensions to fixed levels, LFQ in MAGVIT-v2 [589] uses
binary dimensions to obtain a very large implicit vocabulary, and BSQ [644] projects embeddings onto a
hypersphere before binary quantization. They differ in optimization stability, vocabulary size, and distortion
at a given token budget.
Video tokenization. The video encoder also fixes the spatiotemporal extent of each token and hence the
sequence length. C-ViViT in Phenaki [461] factorizes spatial and temporal encoding into causal discrete
tokens, MAGVIT-v2 pairs a causal tokenizer with LFQ for both autoregressive and masked generation, and
HiTVideo [661] shortens the sequence with hierarchical codes that place semantics and fine detail in different
layers. Fixed grids, however, spend as many tokens on a static shot as on a fast-changing scene. Adaptive
tokenizers allocate the budget by content: EVATok [536] predicts how many tokens each segment needs, and
VideoFlexTok [10] emits a variable-length coarse-to-fine sequence whose early tokens carry semantics and
motion and whose later tokens add detail.
5.2.3 Hybrid and Semantically Aligned Representations
Hybrid tokenizers combine continuous and discrete forms or change the decoder. OneVAE [660] initializes
a discrete video VAE from a continuous one and trains both in one network, so continuous latents can
feed diffusion or flow models and discrete codes can feed sequence models. Divot [122] and DiCoDe [261]
31
