Figure 5 Generative modeling paradigms. Diffusion and flow matching learn denoising or velocity targets over video
latents. Autoregressive modeling factorizes an ordered sequence, while masked prediction fills selected positions from
visible context through parallel conditional predictions.
instead decode with a diffusion model. Divot learns continuous features for LLM-based understanding and
reconstruction, and DiCoDe compresses video into deep tokens that an LLM predicts autoregressively.
Semantic alignment adds a further requirement. A reconstruction-optimal latent may keep texture and local
motion without organizing them into the objects, events, and relations needed for understanding, while
a highly semantic latent may discard the detail that generation needs. VILA-U [521] trains one discrete
tokenizer for both reconstruction and text alignment, and the SEED tokenizer [123] gives a language model a
semantically organized visual code. PyraTok [432] quantizes features at several spatiotemporal scales against
one text-guided codebook, so coarse semantics and fine detail occupy different levels. Section 6 pursues this
question in models that share LLMs, parameters, or objectives across understanding and generation.
Representation autoencoders start from the semantic side: they freeze a pretrained encoder as the tokenizer
and train only a projector or pooler and a decoder. V-RAE [139] compares image and video encoders such
as DINOv3 and V-JEPA 2.1 and finds that reconstruction ranking does not consistently predict generation
quality, so a semantic latent must be tested inside the target generator.
5.3 Generative Video Foundation Modeling
Video generators differ in their generative objective, the order in which space-time units are predicted, and
the system that organizes synthesis. Diffusion, autoregressive or masked prediction, and flow matching define
model-level generation processes (Figure 5), whereas LLM/MLLM-assisted and agentic systems add planning,
tool use, and revision around a visual generator that may use any of the three. The levels combine in practice:
a system may denoise each chunk while rolling out autoregressively in time, and an agent may repeatedly call
a flow-based generator. Preference optimization is applied on top of these generators. VideoReward [284]
aligns a flow-based generator with Flow-DPO on a multidimensional preference signal, and LiFT [484]
applies reward-weighted fine-tuning with a reward model learned from human annotations. World-R1 [473]
uses feedback from pretrained 3D and vision-language models to reinforce geometric consistency through
Flow-GRPO. Table 12 compares representative systems whose technical routes are publicly documented.

5.3.1 Diffusion-Based Generation
Diffusion models learn to reverse a gradual corruption process: a clean video latent is perturbed according
to a noise schedule, and the model learns to predict the noise, a velocity, or an equivalent denoising target
at any noise level [167, 396]. The ϵ- and v-prediction objectives and EDM-style schedules are alternative
parameterizations of this problem (Section 3.3); they affect optimization and sampling, not the video
architecture. Flow-matching generators (Section 5.3.3) share these backbones, so the backbone and long-video
designs below apply to both.
Backbone and attention. Early video diffusion systems, including Video Diffusion Models [169], Imagen
Video [166], and Make-A-Video [417], extended convolutional U-Nets with temporal convolutions or factorized
spatial and temporal attention. Lumiere [19] instead generates the whole clip in one pass with a space-
time U-Net that downsamples in both space and time, avoiding keyframe generation followed by temporal
34


Video Foundation Models
World-Modeling Lens
super-resolution. Diffusion Transformers (DiTs) [369] then made it easier to scale attention over compressed
space-time tokens. CogVideoX [570], Mochi [125], and LTX-Video [145] pair a causal or highly compressed
video VAE with a DiT. CogVideoX uses an expert Transformer with separate text and video normalization,
Mochi gives the visual stream more capacity in an asymmetric DiT, and LTX-Video compresses aggressively so
that full spatiotemporal attention stays affordable. Joint audio-video backbones couple two modality streams
through bidirectional cross-modal attention, so timing and semantics are exchanged throughout synthesis.
LTX-2 [144] extends LTX-Video with separate modality VAEs and asymmetric audio and video streams, and
Ovi [309] couples matched audio and video DiTs trained with flow matching.
The main backbone trade-off concerns attention.
Factorized attention separates spatial and temporal
interactions to save cost, whereas full spatiotemporal attention lets all space-time tokens interact at a higher
memory cost. Sparse and linear attention cut this cost for long or high-resolution clips. HunyuanVideo 1.5 [506]
uses selective and sliding tile attention within a unified T2V and I2V training framework, and SANA-Video
2.0 [53] interleaves gated linear attention with periodic softmax layers and routes block summaries across
depth. Conditions enter the backbone through cross-attention, adaptive normalization such as AdaLN, joint
text-video attention, or channel and token concatenation (Section 5.4).
Streaming and long videos. Standard diffusion denoises a fixed-length clip as a whole, whereas streaming
variants generate causally, chunk by chunk. Diffusion Forcing [44] gives each frame its own noise level,
MAGI-1 [443] denoises chunks autoregressively, CausVid [582] distills a bidirectional model into a few-step
causal generator, Self-Forcing [190] trains on the model’s own rollouts, and Causal Forcing [664] distills from
an autoregressive teacher (Section 7.2.2, Table 17). S2PD [176] instead changes the sampling order within a
clip, generating blocks autoregressively at high noise, then refining the sequence in parallel at low noise.
Long videos raise a different problem: repeated local generation keeps short-term motion but gradually loses
appearance and global structure. StreamingT2V [162] carries anchor frames and short- and long-term memory
across windows, FreeLong [313] blends low-frequency global features with high-frequency local ones during
denoising without training, and FramePack [619] compresses past frames by importance to bound the context.
Other methods change training or the history representation. LongTake [364] supervises later frames from long
real-video prefixes before self-rollout distribution matching, and its evaluation separates sustained motion from
appearance quality, since a nearly static continuation can score well on consistency. Test-time training [77]
stores context in MLP weights that a self-supervised loss updates while the sequence is processed, and has
been demonstrated on one-minute, storyboard-conditioned cartoons.
5.3.2 Autoregressive and Masked Token Generation
Autoregressive video models factorize the distribution over an ordered sequence of video units. With discrete
tokens, this resembles language modeling: VideoGPT [553] paired a VQ-VAE with a Transformer prior over
video codes, CogVideo [170] scaled the recipe to text-conditioned generation, and VideoPoet [217] applied it in
a multimodal sequence model. Loong [487] places text and video tokens in one sequence and uses short-to-long
training and inference-time re-encoding to limit error accumulation in minute-level videos. InfinityStar [285]
extends discrete autoregression to 720p image, video, and long-video generation, reporting about 30 times
lower latency than Wan 2.1-14B in its own single-GPU setting. The route offers a standard causal likelihood
and flexible length, at the cost of long decoding chains, dependence on tokenizer quality, and errors that
propagate through the prefix.
Masked generation relaxes the one-token-at-a-time order: the model predicts sets of masked positions from
visible tokens, often refining coarse-to-fine over several rounds. Phenaki [461] applies a bidirectional masked
Transformer to causal video tokens, MAGVIT-v2 [589] improves the tokenizer and the masked generation
framework, and Lumos-1 [595] keeps frames causal while decoding each frame in parallel with mask-based
discrete diffusion. Continuous tokens support the same factorization. MAR [252] introduced a diffusion loss
for the conditional distribution of a continuous image token, and NOVA [82] and VideoMAR [585] carry it to
video, predicting frames causally in time and continuous tokens within each frame with a diffusion loss.
35

Figure 6 System-level organization for video generation. An LLM or MLLM plans for a visual generator through an
explicit plan, such as shot lists, storyboards, or layouts, or through learned plan embeddings, and agentic systems
close the loop with scripting, generation, evaluation, and revision under a controller.
5.3.3 Flow-Based Generation
Flow matching [280] trains a time-dependent vector field that transports samples from a simple base
distribution to the data along a prescribed probability path, and generation integrates the corresponding
ordinary differential equation. It differs from the usual diffusion account in its training target and sampling
path: rectified-flow formulations favor nearly straight paths, which can reduce the number of solver steps, and
the same DiT or multimodal DiT (MMDiT) can parameterize either target. Stable Diffusion 3 [92] made the
rectified-flow and MMDiT combination prominent for images, and video systems adapted it to space-time
latents. HunyuanVideo [219], Step-Video-T2V [328], Goku [55], and Wan [462] scale flow matching with
DiT backbones and video VAEs, HunyuanVideo with a dual-stream-to-single-stream Transformer. Pyramid
Flow [205] changes the path itself, linking spatial pyramid stages and compressing full-resolution history with
a temporal pyramid so that one DiT is trained end to end across resolutions.
Normalizing flows are a distinct, likelihood-based route that should not be confused with flow matching.
STARFlow-V [136] applies invertible autoregressive transformations to pretrained video-VAE latents, with a
global–local decomposition and a light causal denoiser, so the likelihood is tractable in latent space.
5.3.4 LLM/MLLM-Assisted Generation
LLM/MLLM-assisted generation covers systems in which a language model plans, expands, captions, or
structures the conditions for a separate visual sampler. A language model that predicts video tokens directly
belongs to autoregressive generation instead. In the common arrangement (Figure 6), the language model turns
an underspecified request into scenes, shots, layouts, or refined prompts that a diffusion, flow, or autoregressive
generator consumes. VideoDirectorGPT [273] produces a grounded video plan before layout-conditioned
generation, and LLM-grounded Video Diffusion [265] and Dysen-VDM [99] draft dynamic scene layouts or
scene graphs that guide the denoiser, so the language model handles composition and timing while the video
model handles appearance and motion. Any2Caption [517] interprets text, image, video, region, motion, and
camera cues and translates them into dense structured captions for a downstream generator.
The language model and the generator can also be coupled more tightly than through a written prompt.
MetaCanvas [272] lets an MLLM plan in spatial or spatiotemporal latent spaces connected to diffusion
backbones, including image-to-video and editing settings.
TV2TV [148] couples the two more tightly,
interleaving language next-token prediction with video flow matching in one framework.
5.3.5 Agentic Generation
Agentic generation turns planning into an executable loop: a controller decomposes a request, calls specialized
models or tools, inspects intermediate results, and revises the plan or conditions when the output falls short
(Figure 6). The recurring stages are scripting, storyboarding, visual generation, evaluation, and revision.

Current agents mainly decompose long-form creation or coordinate tools. MovieAgent [519] assigns screenplay,
directing, storyboard, and scene construction to cooperating agents, and Anim-Director [263] has an MLLM
write a storyline and script, produce consistent scene references, and select outputs from generative tools.
Mora [599] routes among generation and editing skills in a multi-agent workflow.
5.4 Control Strategies
Section 5.1 classifies generation by the information supplied; here we classify control by the component through
which that information alters generation. A camera path, pose sequence, or reference image may enter as
input tokens, adapter features, a latent trajectory, sampling guidance, or tuned parameters. This entry point
determines what must be trained, how strong and local the control is, and how easily several signals combine.
Figure 7 locates the entry points relative to the backbone, and Table 13 compares the six families. A single
system often combines several, as in source inversion followed by attention reuse and classifier-free guidance.
5.4.1 Attention-Based Control
Attention-based methods reuse the correspondences that a text-conditioned generator already forms: cross-
attention links prompt tokens to spatial features, and self-attention relates visual features to one another.
Prompt-to-Prompt [163] injects cross-attention maps from a source prompt into the denoising run for an
edited prompt, keeping the layout while changing a word, an attribute, or a concept’s strength.
Video editing also needs the change to stay stable across frames. Video-P2P [293] and FateZero [377] combine
attention control with inversion of the source video. Video-P2P adds separate guidance for source and target
prompts, and FateZero fuses source and target cross- and self-attention maps recorded during inversion.
TokenFlow [126] instead propagates diffusion features between frames through feature-space correspondences,

5.4.2 Adapter-Based Control
Adapters add a trainable path for external structure while keeping most or all of the pretrained generator
frozen. ControlNet [620] copies the encoder blocks of a frozen image diffusion model into a conditional branch
joined by zero-initialized convolutions, whereas T2I-Adapter [347] adds features from a small condition encoder
at several resolutions. They represent the two main variants: a heavy side network that mirrors the backbone
38


Video Foundation Models
World-Modeling Lens
and a light adapter that produces residual features.
Video adapters must also encode how a condition changes over time. MotionCtrl [492] represents camera
motion with poses and object motion with trajectories so that the two can be composed. VideoControlNet [182]
brings ControlNet to video-to-video translation with motion guidance that keeps the injected structure aligned
across frames. Unified adapters train several controls together. VACE [203] encodes reference frames, source
video, and masks in a Video Condition Unit and injects them into a frozen DiT through a context adapter,
supporting reference generation, editing, and masked editing with shared adapter parameters.
5.4.3 Latent-Space Control
Latent-space methods act on the noisy sample or the denoising trajectory itself. Partial noising, as in
SDEdit [340], perturbs an input to an intermediate noise level and denoises it under a new condition, so the
noise level trades fidelity against edit strength. Inversion maps an observed video to a noise trajectory from
which the generator approximately reconstructs it, often through deterministic DDIM sampling [421]; editing
then changes the condition or selected latents along the reverse path.
A method may also construct latent conditions directly. Wan-Move [73] projects dense point trajectories
into latent space and propagates first-frame features along them, giving an image-to-video model a motion
condition without a separate motion encoder. VLIPP [564] has a vision-language model plan coarse object
motion, renders the plan as a synthetic motion video, and converts its optical flow into structured noise that
initializes a motion-controllable image-to-video model (Section 7.2.4).
5.4.4 Concatenation and In-Context Conditioning
The most direct route places the condition in the model input. Channel concatenation appends aligned
condition latents to the noisy video latent, as in the image-to-video variants of Stable Video Diffusion [27]
and CogVideoX [570] and in VideoComposer [475], which fuses spatial and temporal conditions before
concatenation. Token concatenation instead appends condition embeddings to the sequence processed by self-
or joint attention.
FullDiT [207] maps heterogeneous conditions into one sequence and models them with full attention, avoiding
separately trained side branches. In-context generation places reference examples or source–target pairs in the
sequence so that the model infers the requested transformation, as Emu2 [429] showed for multimodal image
generation and UNIC [576] applies to unified video editing. Retrieval can supply further condition tokens:
PhysRAG [69] retrieves videos of relevant physical phenomena, extracts physical information, and injects it
into a video diffusion model through learnable queries.
5.4.5 Guidance-Based Control
Guidance changes the sampling direction instead of adding a persistent condition branch. Classifier-free
guidance (CFG) [168] combines conditional and unconditional predictions at each denoising step,
bϵcfg = ϵθ(zk, k, ∅) + γ
 ϵθ(zk, k, c) −ϵθ(zk, k, ∅)

,
(6)
where zk is the noisy latent at denoising step k, ∅denotes the null condition, and γ is the guidance scale;
γ = 1 recovers the conditional prediction. CFG needs a model trained with condition dropout, and raising γ
usually strengthens alignment but reduces diversity.
More general guidance uses the gradient of an energy, attention, correspondence, or physical objective to
modify each update and can impose constraints absent from the training data. Universal guidance [15] and
training-free energy guidance [588] use external objectives, whereas diffusion self-guidance [91] defines them
on the generator’s internal attention and feature maps.
5.4.6 Tuning-Based Control
Tuning stores a new subject, style, or source-video prior in learned embeddings or weights. For images, Textual
Inversion [112] learns new text embeddings for a user concept, DreamBooth [398] fine-tunes the model on
39


Video Foundation Models
World-Modeling Lens
a few subject images with a prior-preservation loss, and LoRA [174] learns low-rank weight updates while
keeping the base weights fixed. Video adds the requirement that the learned identity stay stable under motion
and viewpoint change.
Tune-A-Video [512] and LoRA-Edit [113] tune on each source video. Tune-A-Video adapts an image diffusion
model from one text–video pair with spatiotemporal attention and DDIM inversion, and LoRA-Edit applies
mask-aware LoRA tuning to an image-to-video model, taking structure and motion from the source and edited
appearance from reference images. Video2LoRA [523] avoids per-reference optimization: a hypernetwork
predicts reference-specific LoRA weights in one forward pass.
5.5 Applications
Generated video serves as content, as an avatar, as a visual plan, or as training data, and each use places
different demands on conditioning, latency, consistency, and evaluation. Systems that also model action-
conditioned transitions are treated in Section 7.
5.5.1 Creative Content Production
Video generation enters production at several stages, from concept shots and storyboards to narrative
footage, advertising and educational variants, and visual-effects edits. The agentic systems of Section 5.3.5
handle planning and tool orchestration, while foundation generators increasingly bundle creation and revision
operations. Following Sora [31], Movie Gen [372] combines text-to-video generation with personalization,
instruction-based editing, and synchronized audio. Closed systems such as Kling [224] and Sora 2 [360]
offer text- and image-to-video generation as products, and Sora 2 adds synchronized audio. Open models
such as VideoCrafter2 [48], Open-Sora [648], Wan [462], HunyuanVideo [219], and Step-Video-T2V [328]
make text-to-video generation available through released weights. Seedance 1.0 [120] adds native multi-shot
generation with consistent subjects, and Seedance 2.0 [442] accepts text, image, audio, and video inputs for
joint audio-video generation, reference-based generation, and editing.
Production also needs what per-clip realism does not measure: keeping a character, location, or style across
shots, revising one element without disturbing the rest, and controlling camera and motion. These needs tie
personalization and control (Section 5.4) to cross-shot and preference evaluation (Section 5.1.2).
5.5.2 Avatars and Character Animation
Avatar generation maps speech, motion, or other control signals to the visible behavior of a person or character.
Its lineage runs through the audio-driven systems of Section 5.1: Wav2Lip [375] edits the mouth region with
a lip-sync expert, and SadTalker [626] predicts expression and head-pose coefficients of a 3D face model.
Diffusion models such as EMO [446] and Hallo [542] generate expressive portrait motion directly from audio,
and VLOGGER [75] extends the output to the upper body with hand and gesture motion from a single
reference image. Pose-driven animation, as in Animate Anyone [179] and MagicAnimate [548], replaces audio
with a motion sequence and is judged against that motion instead of speech. In both settings, identity,
clothing, background, and non-speech motion must stay stable over long or varied inputs.
Real-time avatars must also produce frames at a sustained rate with bounded delay while staying synchronized
with incoming audio or motion. MuseTalk [633] dubs video in latent space and reports 30 frames per second
at 256 × 256 resolution on a V100 GPU. Vidu S1 [616] lets voice instructions redirect an ongoing character
stream, and Vidu S2 [615] adds references that can be updated during streaming and frame-aligned editing of
an incoming video stream. Mid-stream control adds action-to-output latency to throughput as a criterion and
links avatars to the real-time interactive world models of Section 7.2.3.
5.5.3 Video Plans for Embodied Agents
Generated video can serve as a plan between a task specification and physical execution, and methods differ
mainly in how they recover actions from it. UniPi [87] generates a future video from a text task and extracts
controls with an inverse dynamics model, using images as a planning space shared by embodiments with
different action spaces. AVDC [215] infers actions from dense correspondences between generated frames, so
40


Video Foundation Models
World-Modeling Lens
policies can be learned from videos without action labels. RoboDreamer [656] composes video predictions
of reusable instruction primitives to generalize to new object–action combinations, and Dreamitate [269]
fine-tunes a video model on human demonstrations and tracks the generated tool motion to control a robot.
A generated plan stays fixed, whereas an interactive simulator such as UniSim [561] updates its predictions as
actions arrive (Section 7).
5.5.4 Synthetic Data for Embodied AI and Driving
Embodied and driving systems need observations of rare tasks, environments, and failures that are costly or
unsafe to collect. Video generation can supply them offline by varying appearance, layout, traffic, camera pose,
or actions while keeping the annotations carried by the condition. In driving, MagicDrive [116] conditions
multi-camera street views on camera poses, road maps, 3D bounding boxes, and text with cross-view attention,
and Panacea [503] generates annotated multi-view videos from bird’s-eye-view layouts. DriveDreamer [476]
and GAIA-1 [172] go further and predict future driving observations under scene or action conditions
(Section 7). In robotics, the data must also carry actions: DreamGen [198] adapts an image-to-video model to
a target embodiment and labels its generated trajectories with a latent-action or inverse-dynamics model.
GigaWorld-0 [441] pairs a controllable video generator with 3D generation and system identification to train
vision-language-action policies (Section 7). Generated data are judged by downstream perception and policy
results on real observations, not by visual fidelity.
­ INSIGHT Rendering fidelity and stateful control are distinct scaling axes
Video priors increasingly make each clip convincing, yet visual fidelity and stateful control are separate goals. A
good control changes only its target, keeps that change over time, and combines with other controls without
disturbing identity, geometry, or motion. Progress should therefore be measured by comparing matched rollouts
with and without the control, not by the quality of each rollout alone.
6
T Unified Video Models: Sharing the World
Sharing means understanding and generation use common representations, parameters, interfaces, or objectives.
Video understanding and generation have traditionally relied on separate encoders and generators. Unified
video models implement the sharing relation V ↔W of Section 2: inferred content can condition generation,
and generative structure can support understanding and reasoning.
Definition 2.6 evaluates sharing at three levels: capability coverage (one system performs both directions),
architectural sharing (the directions use common components), and functional synergy (one direction improves
the other under a controlled comparison). We cover systems with video input or output that link understanding
and generation, and cite image-only work where it supplies a design or evaluation protocol missing for video.
6.1 Unified Architectures
Unified architectures differ in where understanding and generation meet (Figure 8): in a shared token sequence,
a shared continuous latent space, a semantic bridge between decoupled experts, or the task state of an agent
that calls specialized tools. Table 14 compares representative video systems along the component dimensions
of Zhao et al. [640]. The first three families have image-domain precedents: Chameleon [439] for early-fusion
token sequences, Transfusion [650] for autoregressive text and diffusion images in one Transformer, Janus [508]
for decoupled encoders, and MetaQueries [363] for learned-query bridges. On the video side, UniVL [315]
earlier unified video–language understanding and caption generation, though its generated output was text,
not video.

6.1.1 Discrete-Token Unification
Discrete-token models convert text, images, and video into symbols that are interleaved in one sequence. A
single Transformer then serves both directions with next-token prediction: answering predicts text tokens
after video context, and generation predicts visual tokens after a text or visual condition. Emu3 [479] follows
this design without diffusion heads, covering image and video understanding and generation, and Emu3.5 [76]
scales the same objective to video-heavy data and reports world-modeling behavior such as spatiotemporally
43


Video Foundation Models
World-Modeling Lens
consistent exploration. Video-LaVIT [206] cuts the token cost of video by tokenizing keyframes and motion
separately, and VILA-U [521] trains its vision tower to align discrete tokens with language while keeping the
detail needed for generation. SEED-LLaMA [123] pairs discrete visual tokens with a diffusion image decoder;
it answers video questions but generates only images. Two variants change the role of the tokens or the
objective: Vega [486] predicts semantic keyframe tokens autoregressively and leaves appearance to a diffusion
renderer, and NExT-OMNI [323] replaces autoregression with discrete flow matching over text, image, video,
and audio tokens.
This route gives many input–output combinations one vocabulary, one sequence format, and one likelihood
objective. However, the tokenizer becomes the bottleneck: codes must preserve appearance and motion
without making sequences unmanageably long, and quantization discards information before the Transformer
sees it. Long autoregressive outputs also accumulate errors through their generated prefix.
6.1.2 Continuous Latent-Space Unification
Continuous latent-space models avoid quantization: an encoder maps images or video into a continuous feature
space that an understanding head reads and a decoder, diffusion model, or flow head turns back into pixels.
The latent space is shared, but the losses and output heads can differ, because language prediction and pixel
generation have different numerical requirements. TUNA [306] cascades a VAE encoder with a representation
encoder, and Show-o2 [531] replaces the discrete vocabulary of Show-o [530] with a 3D causal VAE; both predict
text autoregressively and generate images and video with flow matching. Uni-ViGU [382] starts from the other
end, extending a video diffusion generator with discrete flow matching for text and lightweight understanding
layers. AToken [311] and HYDRA-X [609] make the tokenizer itself the shared component, training it with
reconstruction and semantic objectives over images, video, and, for AToken, 3D. PixelUMM [499] drops the
pretrained encoder and VAE: pixel patches and video tubelets enter a mixture-of-transformers in which the
two directions keep separate projections and feed-forward layers but share attention, supporting video question
answering, generation, and editing.
Continuous spaces fit diffusion and flow generators naturally, but they expose a tension that recurs across
unified models. Understanding benefits from invariance to texture and small appearance changes, whereas
generation must retain enough detail to reconstruct them.
6.1.3 Decoupled and Modular Unification
Decoupled systems sidestep this tension by keeping separate visual pathways for the two directions, linked
through an LLM, a projection layer, or a learned adapter. The understanding encoder can drop nuisance
detail and emphasize objects, relations, actions, or text, while a separate tokenizer or decoder keeps the
fine spatial and temporal detail needed to generate video. NExT-GPT [516] and GPT4Video [489] set the
modular baseline around an instruction-following LLM and frozen pretrained encoders and generators, so only
a small part of the system is trained: NExT-GPT links the LLM to diffusion decoders through lightweight
projections, whereas GPT4Video has the LLM write textual prompts for an off-the-shelf text-to-video model.
Later systems train the bridge. Omni-Video [435], UniVid [317], and UniVideo [498] let an MLLM interpret
the instruction and pass continuous features through an adapter or bridge to a diffusion decoder; UniVid adds
a reflection mechanism for temporal reasoning. Omni-Video 2 [557] keeps the MLLM frozen and conditions
the decoder on a predicted target caption and source-video latents for editing, and ReViSE [298] trains an
Omni-Video model with feedback from its own VLM (Section 6.2.4).
Other systems redesign the bridge itself. Lumos-Nexus [534] aligns a small generator with the understanding
model and mixes its velocity predictions with those of a larger pretrained generator at inference. MoRoute [114]
learns which layer of a frozen VLM conditions each diffusion block, and Mamoda2.5 [413] feeds semantic features
to a sparse-expert diffusion backbone through learned queries. Lance [111] replaces the bridge with shared
attention: separate semantic and VAE encoders feed dual experts that attend over one interleaved sequence.
JavisGPT [287] extends the encoder–LLM–decoder pattern to sounding video, passing synchrony-aware queries
to a joint audio–video generator.
Decoupling keeps strong pretrained components and makes each one replaceable. However, the bridge can
become an information bottleneck: a semantic clue sufficient for following an instruction may omit the

Figure 9 Learning paradigms in unified video models. Isolated learning keeps independently pretrained experts; dual
learning relates inverse tasks or reconstruction cycles; co-learning optimizes both directions jointly; and synergistic
learning targets cross-direction benefit. The paradigms can coexist.

6.1.4 Agentic and Tool-Augmented Unification
Agentic systems leave perception, generation, editing, and evaluation to specialized models. A controller reads
the request and media, plans operations, calls tools, inspects their outputs, and revises the plan; what is
shared is a task state such as a script, shot plan, prompt, edit graph, or critique. VideoAgent (editing) [653]
plans shots and retrieves source clips before calling a library of editing agents, and VISTA [307] wraps a video
generator in a loop of temporal planning, candidate selection, multimodal critique, and prompt rewriting.
UniVA [270] plans and calls understanding, generation, editing, and segmentation tools that share global,
task, and user memories.
Agents can combine strong tools without forcing their internal representations to agree, and each tool can
be swapped as better models appear. However, they depend on tool selection, intermediate formats, and
evaluator reliability: one wrong caption, retrieval result, or critique can redirect the whole workflow.
6.2 Learning Paradigms
Architecture fixes where understanding and generation meet; training determines what signal flows between
them (Figure 9). Isolated learning connects independently trained experts, dual learning relates the two direc-
tions through inverse or cycle objectives, co-learning optimizes both over shared parameters or representations,
and synergistic learning uses one direction to improve the other. The paradigms can coexist; for example, a
cycle objective can be part of joint training.
6.2.1 Isolated Learning
Isolated learning trains understanding and generation as independent experts and connects them after
pretraining through a small trained module, while most expert parameters stay frozen. NExT-GPT [516]
45


Video Foundation Models
World-Modeling Lens
and GPT4Video [489] follow this recipe (Section 6.1), as GILL [216] did for a frozen language model and a
frozen image generator. Isolated learning is cheap and modular, and the source of each capability stays easy
to identify. However, generation losses never update the video encoder and understanding losses never update
the decoder, so no cross-direction transfer can occur, which makes isolated systems the natural baseline for
measuring what tighter coupling adds.
6.2.2 Dual Learning
Dual learning treats understanding and generation as approximately inverse problems: a model reconstructs a
condition from generated content, regenerates an observation from an inferred representation, or enforces
agreement over a round trip. Such objectives let one direction supervise the other when paired labels are
scarce. As many videos fit one description and one condition allows many valid futures, a video cycle must
preserve task-relevant events, identities, viewpoints, and motion while tolerating appearance variation.
Uni-ViGU [382] is the clearest video-native example. Starting from a video generator, it recovers understanding
as the inverse task: it first reconstructs prompts to recall learned text–video associations and then trains
on detailed captions, keeping the original generation path. Dedicated cycle objectives remain image-based:
CyCLeGen [405] couples image–layout cycles, UniCorn [98] distills text–image–text self-play among proposer,
solver, and judge roles, and Reconstruction Alignment [529] trains a model to reconstruct an image from its
own understanding embeddings.
6.2.3 Co-learning
Co-learning optimizes understanding and generation objectives in one shared model or a jointly trained bridge,
and each of the first three families of Section 6.1 supports it. Discrete-token models such as Emu3 [479],
Video-LaVIT [206], and VILA-U [521] apply one next-token objective to both directions, while Vega [486]
shapes a shared semantic token space and keeps a separate diffusion renderer. Continuous and hybrid models
share a visual space or Transformer but keep distinct losses, as in TUNA [306], Show-o2 [531], HYDRA-X [609],
PixelUMM [499], and the single-Transformer HaploOmni [527]. BAGEL [81] co-trains a mixture of transformer
experts that share attention but keep direction-specific projections and feed-forward layers, a design Lance
applies to video (Section 6.1). Bridge models such as Omni-Video [435], UniVid [317], and UniVideo [498]
co-train the adapter between an MLLM and a video decoder.
Joint training creates an optimization imbalance between the two objectives. Understanding data rewards
semantic invariance and concise answers, whereas generation data rewards retaining appearance and motion
detail. Differences in dataset scale, loss magnitude, and convergence speed can let one direction dominate
the shared parameters, so the data mixture and schedule decide how strongly each capability updates the
model. Lance [111] counters this interference with staged multitask training and adaptive data scheduling.
Tong et al. [452] study related choices when pretraining a Transfusion-style backbone from scratch, comparing
representation autoencoders, data mixtures, and dense or sparse-expert architectures with compute-matched
image–text analyses.
6.2.4 Synergistic Learning
Synergistic learning uses one direction to improve the other; its evidence is a measured gain in the receiving
direction. From understanding to generation, OmniWeaving [362] conditions a video generator on an MLLM
that can first write a reasoning trace about interleaved text, image, and video inputs; after end-to-end training,
enabling this thinking mode raises its scores on intent-driven and interpolative tasks of IntelligentVBench.
ReViSE [298] turns understanding into a training signal for editing: the frozen VLM inside an Omni-
Video model scores a one-step estimate of the edited video, and its answer-token probabilities serve as a
differentiable reward alongside flow matching. Ablations against supervised fine-tuning on RVE-Bench support
this understanding-to-editing transfer. Lance’s data-mixture ablations [111] give video evidence in both
directions: adding understanding data improves generation on VBench, and adding generation data improves
understanding on MVBench.
From generation to understanding, Uni-ViGU [382] transfers a generator’s text–video associations through
inverse-task training (Section 6.2.2), and Thinking with Video [451] generates intermediate frames at inference
46


Video Foundation Models
World-Modeling Lens
(Section 6.3.2). CounterVid [374] transfers through data: it synthesizes counterfactual action clips and trains
a video-language model with a preference objective, reducing action and temporal hallucinations more than
generic visual negatives do. UnityVideo [185] jointly learns depth, flow, segmentation, pose, and other visual
conditions, improving both world-aware generation and the prediction of those signals. Gen4U [213] and
GenCeption [468] reuse frozen or adapted video diffusion features for perception (Section 4.2).
Integrated tasks provide a third kind of evidence. UniUGP [310] tests whether scene reasoning and predicted
video improve driving trajectories within one system (Section 6.3.3). EWAM [466] shows that masking or
counterfactually replacing predicted-future features changes a manipulation policy’s actions and success. Tong
et al. [452] also find that general multimodal pretraining data, especially raw video, helps offline navigation
planning through action-conditioned prediction. Apart from Lance’s data-mixture ablations, controlled
bidirectional tests remain image-based (Section 6.4).
6.3 Applications
Applications test whether information passed between understanding and generation improves a task outcome.
6.3.1 Instruction-Driven Video Editing and Creation
Instruction-driven editing follows a read–modify–write pattern: the model identifies the entities, events,
motion, and time span an instruction refers to, then changes them while preserving everything else. A
plausible output is still wrong if it edits the wrong interval, loses subject identity, or damages surrounding
content. Unified models place both steps in one system: Omni-Video [435] and UniVideo [498] pass MLLM
interpretations to a diffusion decoder (Section 6.1), and UNIC [576] casts editing as in-context generation over
one shared sequence. ReViSE [298] adds reasoning to editing data: it pairs reasoning-enriched instructions
with target edits and separates modifying a clip from generating a plausible continuation.
Agentic systems extend editing into production workflows: VideoAgent (editing) [653] plans and assembles
multi-shot edits, and VISTA [307] uses its own critiques to rewrite prompts across rounds (Section 6.1).
Evaluation should therefore score localization, preservation of untouched content, and improvement across
rounds, not only the quality of the final video.
6.3.2 Generative Reasoning
Generative reasoning uses generation as an intermediate computation. A video can show a changing configu-
ration that is hard to express in one image or a textual chain of thought, as objects move, occlusions change,
and the consequences of an operation unfold. The generated sequence then serves as an inspectable hypothesis
about the process. Thinking with Video [451] uses a video generator to produce intermediate frames during
multimodal reasoning and evaluates them on VideoThinkBench. OpenCoF [58] trains chain-of-frame genera-
tion on temporally organized reasoning videos, while MME-CoF-Pro [321, 378] shows that answer accuracy
can rise while intermediate frames stay inconsistent or hallucinated. ProAR [408] makes autoregressive video
diffusion prospective by predicting its own goal frame alongside each chunk.
However, generated reasoning traces add a failure mode: a coherent but unsupported intermediate video can
make a later answer look well grounded. Reliable use thus needs observed evidence separated from generated
hypotheses, and tests of whether the intermediate video changes the answer for the right reason.
6.3.3 Unified Perception-Generation-Planning Systems
In planning, generated video predicts what an action will do: the system reads the current observation,
predicts how the scene may evolve under a plan, and uses that prediction to choose or refine the action. Its
value lies in the resulting decision, not in visual quality. UniUGP [310] applies this loop to autonomous
driving: from multi-frame observations and instructions, its hybrid experts produce scene reasoning, future
video, and a trajectory, connected through a four-stage curriculum. The question is whether future generation
improves planning in difficult or long-tail situations. VideoAgent (planning) [423] applies the same idea to
embodied tasks, refining generated video plans through self-conditioning and execution feedback.

Recent models extend sharing to actions. Cosmos 3 [354] places language, image, video, audio, and action
sequences in one omnimodal mixture-of-transformers. UniWAM [422] couples a physical reasoner, a video
generator, and an action predictor through joint attention, and perturbs predicted future latents during post-
training so that actions do not over-rely on imagined futures. EWAM [466] tests whether a predicted-future
pathway matters for action (Section 6.2.4), and Section 7 details action-conditioned prediction.
6.4 Evaluation
Table 15 expands the sharing row of Table 2 into evaluation levels: separate competence, integrated behavior,
and measured synergy. Video coverage is mature at the first level, emerging at the second, and mostly
directional at the third. Levels 1 and 2 refine the capability-coverage level of Definition 2.6 and Level 3 its
functional-synergy level; architectural sharing is a property of the design (Section 6.1), not a benchmark score,
and decision utility is covered by the integrated tasks of Section 6.2.4.
6.4.1 Per-Direction Evaluation
Each direction is evaluated with its own community’s protocols:
MVBench [248], Video-MME [109],
LongVideoBench [509], LVBench [472], and MLVU [654] for understanding (Section 4.1), and VBench [193]
and VBench-2.0 [647] for generation (Section 5.1.2). Results should be reported separately, since perceptual
and faithfulness criteria are not interchangeable with question-answer accuracy. Matched single-task baselines
can also reveal interference, where joint training improves one direction but degrades the other.
6.4.2 Unified, Interleaved, and Synergy Evaluation
Integrated benchmarks test whether one system handles a wider family of input–output transformations.
UniVBench [501] is the most direct video-centered example: it evaluates understanding, generation, editing,
and reconstruction on multi-shot videos with a shared agentic evaluator, and its reconstruction and editing
tasks check whether source content survives. RVE-Bench [298] and VR-Bench [534] test whether reasoning-
conditioned edits and generations do what the inferred intent implies, and UniVA-Bench [270] scores agentic
workflows on both final media and planning behavior. UniM [257] extends input and output to text, image,
video, and audio, while MME-Unify [533] covers integrated tasks in image–text settings.
Synergy evaluation instead compares matched routes, such as understanding-guided generation against
plain generation, or understanding with and without generated intermediates. Image-domain studies lead
here: DreamLLM [85] was an early study of synergy between understanding and generation in interleaved
48


Video Foundation Models
World-Modeling Lens
documents, and Uni-MMMU [668], RealUnify [412], and UniSandbox [353] score both directions with coupled
tasks, matched routes, or decoupled tracks. UniG2U-Bench [504] finds that an extra generation step helps
understanding on tasks that need visual transformation but can be neutral or harmful elsewhere. Pseudo-
Unification [562] probes internal information patterns and finds divergent processing behind nominally shared
parameters. Video evidence so far comes from training ablations and interventions and is mostly directional
(Section 6.2.4); Section 8.3 discusses what a video synergy test would need.
6.4.3 Protocols and Consistency
Round-trip evaluation tests whether content survives an understanding–generation cycle: a model infers an
event or layout, renders it, and reads it back, or generates a video, describes it, and regenerates it. Existing
round-trip training objectives are image-based (Section 6.2.2), and the reconstruction task of UniVBench is
the closest video protocol. A video protocol must name entities, event order, geometry, or dynamics to keep,
score intended changes separately, and add non-unified and no-cycle controls.
Reliable measurement also needs information controls.
Train–test overlap can confound transfer with
memorization, intermediate representations can leak answers across directions, and text accuracy, video
fidelity, and cross-modal consistency may rely on different, possibly biased judges. UniSandbox [353] reduces
leakage with synthetic tasks and separated modules, TorchUMM [324] standardizes model interfaces and task
implementations, and UniSAFE [232] evaluates safety across input–output modality combinations. Judges
can themselves be unified: UnifiedReward [485] trains one reward model to rank and score image and video
outputs for both understanding and generation.
­ INSIGHT Synergy requires controlled cross-direction transfer
Shared tokens or parameters show coupling, separate scores show competence, and mixed input–output tasks
show interoperability. Synergy needs a controlled comparison: ablate the cross-direction path under matched
data, capacity, and compute, and measure the change in generation, understanding, or an integrated task.
7
 Video World Models: Interacting with the World
Interacting means modeling state transition itself: how an established world evolves over time and, when actions,
controls, or new observations are supplied, how they change that evolution.
Interacting is the operation W ⟲of Section 2: the model advances its state, st+1 ∼pθ(st+1 | s≤t, at, ct),
and decodes observations when visible output is needed. Rollout exposes failures that a single plausible clip
hides: generated errors feed back as context, off-screen content is forgotten, smooth motion breaks physical or
causal structure, and actions miss their intended effects. We first place current models in the taxonomy of
Section 3.4, then examine control, memory, responsiveness, grounding, applications, and evaluation. Zhu et
al. [665] survey general world models starting from Sora; this chapter focuses on video-centered state.
7.1 Architectures and Training Paradigms
Crossing the two axes of Section 3.4 gives the 2 × 3 grid of Figure 10. A cell describes how a model is
trained and used, so one model can occupy several cells: an action-conditioned latent predictor can score fixed
candidate actions or run inside a planner that acts, observes, and replans. Likewise, the attention pattern
does not decide the column; a model is interactive when it accepts new controls between emitted frames or
chunks. Table 16 lists representative implementations, one row per evaluated protocol.
Training data. Each cell needs data that expose the transition it learns. Passive predictors such as V-JEPA 2
and V-JEPA 2.1 pretrain on large image–video mixtures [9, 348], in which video supplies temporal change but
no actions. Action-conditioned learning needs synchronized observations and controls, such as the DROID
robot demonstrations [209] that train the action-conditioned V-JEPA predictors. Open X-Embodiment [357]
pools robot data across embodiments in one format, although action units and coordinate frames still need
alignment.
Annotated video adds further supervision: camera geometry and depth (OmniWorld [659],

Figure 10 The 2 × 3 taxonomy of video world models. Rows give the prediction space: observations (frames, video
tokens, or video-decoding latents) or latent states (video-derived features predicted without required rendering).
Columns give the transition: passive prediction without action input, action-conditioned prediction with controls fixed
before prediction, and interactive prediction that receives new controls after feedback. Each cell shows its prediction
form and representative models; x denotes observations, z features, a actions, H the horizon, and C and Q the context
and target positions of masked feature prediction.

(WildWorld [264]), and recorded intervention branches (CG-World [36]).
7.1.1 Observation-Space Passive Prediction
Passive visual models predict future observations from history alone. They can represent uncertainty over
several plausible futures, as the action-free setting of SV2P [11] does by sampling latent variables to predict
multiple future frames. Large video generators used as forecasters also belong here: Sora [31] was presented
as a step toward world simulation, Physics-IQ [346] scores how such generators continue a real initial segment,
and the base video-to-world models of Cosmos [355] predict futures from a video prefix before any action-
conditioned post-training. Training targets the next frame or a future segment with reconstruction, likelihood,
adversarial, diffusion, or flow losses. Autoregressive models feed their predictions back as history, whereas block
predictors generate a pending segment jointly. Passive prediction supplies dynamics priors and uncertainty
estimates for the controlled cells.
7.1.2 Observation-Space Action-Conditioned Prediction
This cell receives the controls for a whole predicted segment before generation, such as ego motion, a
robot trajectory, camera poses, road layout, or planned object states. GAIA-1 [172] predicts discrete image
tokens conditioned on text and action tokens before decoding video, and DrivingWorld [180] predicts driving
observations together with ego state. Conditions range from ego dynamics, agent configurations, and road
semantics (GAIA-2 [399]) and commands, goal points, and trajectories (Vista [118]) to frame-level robot actions
(IRASim [663]). DriveDreamer [476] and post-trained Cosmos variants [355] condition on bird’s-eye-view
maps, 3D boxes, and camera configurations.
Most models in this cell adapt a pretrained video generator through condition encoders, cross-attention, or
control branches, and add structure, multi-view, or action-fidelity losses to the visual objective. Making them
interactive requires causal generation that accepts new controls during rollout. Vid2World [189] makes a
video diffusion model autoregressive and action-guided, minWM [638] combines control tuning, autoregressive
training, and few-step distillation, and BiWM [397] keeps bidirectional computation within causal generation.

7.1.3 Observation-Space Interactive World Models
Interactive visual models generate causally: emitted frames become history, and each new action changes
the future that has not yet been generated. Genie [32] predicts video tokens from learned latent actions,
GameNGen [459] diffuses the next game frame from past frames and actions, and DIAMOND [4] both trains
policies inside its generated observations and runs as an interactive simulator. GameFactory [587] extends
playable generation to newly constructed scenes, and Matrix-Game [631] couples visual context to keyboard
and mouse input, with a later version [156] adding chunked, few-step streaming diffusion. Oasis [79] renders a
playable Minecraft-like world from keyboard and mouse input in real time, Hunyuan-GameCraft [244] maps
keyboard and mouse actions into a shared camera representation and conditions on hybrid history to keep
long play consistent, and Genie 3 [367] generates worlds from text prompts that users navigate in real time
for several minutes.
Controls can also be speech, and several actors can share one world. Vidu S1 and S2 [615, 616] take speech
and updated references as controls on a streaming character (Section 5.5), and Wan-Streamer [188] keeps
audio-visual context to respond full-duplex to incoming events (Section 7.2.3). Solaris [402] pairs synchronized
actions and observations from two Minecraft players, and MultiWorld [510] binds actions to agent identities
and updates a shared geometry-aware representation between chunks, so that every actor affects the same
world.
7.1.4 Latent-State Passive Prediction
Latent passive models predict video-derived features without actions, often as pretraining for later transition
models. V-JEPA [20] learns by predicting the features of masked video regions, and the pretraining stage
51


Video Foundation Models
World-Modeling Lens
of V-JEPA 2 [9] scales this objective over images and video. Because the masked region can be predicted
from frames on both sides, this objective is not causal forecasting; future prediction requires a separate,
causal protocol. We keep these models in the taxonomy because their predictors supply the feature space and
initialization of the action-conditioned latent cells; on their own, they are pretraining for transition models
rather than complete world models. Later variants change the prediction unit or the loss. Causal-JEPA [349]
infers masked object-level latents from surrounding objects to encourage relational prediction. V-JEPA
2.1 [348] adds a dense context-token loss and multi-level supervision for stronger local structure.
7.1.5 Latent-State Action-Conditioned Prediction
Adding actions keeps a feature-space target but conditions its evolution on controls. V-JEPA 2-AC [9]
post-trains a predictor on robot trajectories, DINO-WM [652] learns how frozen DINOv2 features evolve
under actions, and LeWorldModel [334] instead learns the encoder and predictor jointly from pixels with
latent regularization. V-JEPA 2.1 also trains a diffusion predictor over its features for navigation [348].
Such models can roll a candidate action sequence forward and compare the result with a goal representation
without rendering a single frame. LaDi-WM [192] predicts geometric and semantic features for a manipulation
policy, and MoWM [593] combines motion-aware latent prediction with pixel-space signals. The JEPA-WM
design study [444] compares encoders, proprioception, context, multi-step losses, and planning optimizers in
one recipe and finds that the best combination depends on the task.
7.1.6 Latent-State Interactive World Models
An interactive latent system closes the loop with model predictive control (MPC): the planner chooses an action
prefix, executes it, encodes the new observation, and plans again. V-JEPA 2-AC [9] and DINO-WM [652]
use this loop, and V-JEPA 2.1 [348] applies it to reaching, grasping, and pick-and-place on a real robot arm.
H-JEPA [625] stacks latent spaces at coarser timescales so that upper levels supply subgoals to lower-level
planning. Latent rollout spends no capacity on texture and scores candidates without a decoder, but latent
distance can omit task-relevant details. Because corrective replanning can hide poor scores, scorer-level audits
complement closed-loop success (Section 7.4).
­ INSIGHT Rendering, causal rollout, and decision state can be specialized
A visual generator can supply appearance and motion priors, an online generator can provide action-conditioned
rollout, and a latent predictor can supply compact state for planning. These components need not share a
representation or training stage, but for the same history and action their rendered consequences, retained state,
and decision scores must agree.
7.2 Core Technical Challenges
Four challenges recur across the grid: control, memory, latency, and physical grounding. Memory and physical
consistency matter in every cell; controlled prediction must also bind each control to its consequence, and
interaction must do so within a response budget. The challenges interact: longer memory raises inference cost,
fewer denoising steps change rollout stability, and stronger action constraints can disturb the visual prior
inherited from pretraining.
7.2.1 Action-Conditioned Controllability
Action control poses two problems. Without action records, a latent-action model must learn a control variable
from observed transitions; with supplied controls, conditioning must bind each signal to the right time, view,
object, and consequence.
Latent action learning. Latent-action models couple inverse and forward dynamics. An inverse model
infers a variable that explains the change between two nearby frames, and a forward model predicts the
later frame from the earlier one and that variable. A discrete codebook or continuous bottleneck limits how
much information the variable carries, which favors a compact control code. LAPO [403], LAPA [574], and
Moto [61] use discrete latent actions to pretrain policies from unlabeled video, and Genie [32] uses them
52


Video Foundation Models
World-Modeling Lens
to make platformer videos playable. LAPA then maps its codes to robot commands with a small labeled
set, while Moto treats them as a language of motion tokens. Continuous latent actions offer more capacity.
AdaWorld [119] conditions an autoregressive world model on them and adapts to new action spaces with little
interaction, while IGOR [57] encodes initial-to-goal image changes in one space for humans and robots.
However, a latent action can lock onto the wrong source of change. LAOM [351] shows that visual distractors
lead latent actions to encode background or camera motion, whereas a little action supervision anchors them
to the agent. Latent codes should therefore be tested for decoding into executable actions and for transfer
under distractors, not only for reconstruction. Action Forcing [430] avoids an unconstrained code: it recovers
signed egomotion axes from principal components of tracked pixel motion and uses them as controls that can
be tested for reversal, scaling, and composition.
Action conditioning mechanisms. Known actions remove the discovery problem but must still be aligned with
the frames they affect. A single sequence embedding is too coarse when each frame must respond to a different
command, so IRASim [663] injects actions frame by frame throughout its diffusion Transformer. Other systems
express controls where their effects appear. EA-WM [568] projects joint or end-effector kinematics into the
camera view as visual action fields, and Aero-World [388] feeds acceleration and angular velocity as tokens
and checks inertial consistency with a frozen latent physics probe. X-World [646] conditions multi-camera
driving on ego actions plus optional controls over traffic agents, road elements, and appearance. Models that
predict both states and actions must also stay responsive to actions; FLEX-WAM [210] rebalances the two
losses and tracks an action-sensitivity proxy for this purpose.
7.2.2 Long-Horizon Consistency and Memory
Long-horizon failure occurs at two scales. Locally, small prediction errors return as context and accumulate
across frames or chunks; globally, a finite context drops entities, layouts, and events that are no longer
visible. The first calls for aligning training histories with deployment, the second for storing, compressing, or
recurrently updating information beyond the active window. TECO [552] is an early example, compressing
past frames into temporally consistent latent codes to keep long rollouts coherent.
Training-inference alignment: the forcing family. Under teacher forcing, the model predicts from clean
ground-truth history, whereas at deployment it conditions on its own outputs, so a small error in geometry or
identity becomes the next input. Diffusion Forcing [44] exposes the model to uncertain histories by giving
sequence elements independent noise levels; GameNGen similarly perturbs its conditioning frames with
noise, and LIVE [187] adds forward–reverse cycle consistency. MAGI-1 [443] denoises chunks block-causally.
Autoregressive diffusion adds a second mismatch, between a many-step bidirectional teacher and a few-step
causal student. CausVid [582] makes a pretrained video diffusion model causal and distills its sampler through
distribution matching, Self-Forcing [190] trains the student on its own rollouts, and Causal Forcing [664]
distills from an autoregressive teacher whose sampling path already follows causal inference. Later variants
change the denoising window (Rolling Forcing [290]), the prediction horizon (Next Forcing [537]), or the
distillation objective (One-Forcing [104], Delta Forcing [522]); Table 17 lists their mechanisms and intended
effects.
Retrieval-augmented and structured memory. Retrieval extends the context with stored observations that
are recalled when the camera or agent returns to a relevant region. WorldMem [528] stores frames with pose
and time metadata and attends to them to reconstruct earlier scenes; Context-as-Memory [586] retrieves only
scene-relevant past views, and WorldWeaver [305] builds its memory on predicted depth, which drifts less
than RGB. Spatial Memory Intelligence [566] uses a multimodal LLM to cluster, sparsify, filter, and retrieve
stored views by action, while Keepsake [1] caps the archive by evicting views that others can replace. Such
archives trade growing storage and retrieval cost for detailed evidence that can be revisited.
Geometry makes recall more precise and reduces false matches between similar-looking places. VMem [251]
indexes views by 3D surfels, RELIC [171] stores compressed history with relative actions and absolute camera
poses in its key–value (KV) cache, and AnchorWeave [495] retrieves local memories around persistent anchors.
Long-term spatial memory [518] keeps a reconstructed static point map for layout, recent frames for motion,
and retrieved past frames for appearance. Matrix-Game 3.5 [381] keeps geometrically projected latent patches
for the static scene and reference tokens for moving subjects, so that past positions of an object are not
mistaken for additional objects.

However, remembering what was seen differs from tracking what changes off-screen. WorldDirector [465]
conditions causal chunks on externally planned object and camera trajectories plus retrieved object crops, so
planned objects keep moving while unseen. LiveWorld [90] keeps a static background and localized dynamic
entities and uses virtual monitors to generate their progress outside the view before projecting it back into
the scene; its LiveBench checks this off-screen progression.
Context compression. Compression keeps a bounded working context. FramePack [619] gives older frames
smaller representation budgets than recent ones and adds drift prevention to sampling.
Fixed-size state representations. Fixed-size memories keep a constant-size state and pay a compression cost
at every update. Long-context state-space world models [371] pair a block-wise state-space scan with dense
local attention, and Echo-Infinity [26] recurrently updates memory queries as frames leave its local window.
GIM-World [502] encodes selected past latents into geometry-supervised tokens, and Owl-1 [191] keeps learned
state queries inside a multimodal LLM alongside predicted textual dynamics. Honeycomb [411] recurrently
writes six fixed-size spatial and spatiotemporal feature planes that cover larger ranges at coarser resolution.
WorldTrace [520] keeps a fixed-size KV memory of averaged scene summaries and selected landmark keys that
later queries can reach beyond the recent window, and evaluates it with its LoopBench revisit test. Recurrent
states can drift, test-time weight updates [77] add optimization latency, and compact memories may drop
details needed later for rendering. Retrieval, compression, and fixed state thus trade capacity against latency
differently and can be combined (Table 18).
7.2.3 Real-Time Responsiveness
Responsiveness is the delay between a new action and the first output that reflects it; a slow transition breaks
the control loop even when offline throughput is high. End-to-end cost combines sampling depth, work per step,
active history, cache construction, latent decoding, and the rate at which controls enter the rollout. Recent
systems report real-time rates: Vidu S1 [616] reaches up to 42 frames per second (FPS) at 540p on consumer
GPUs, and Wan-Streamer [188] reaches 25 FPS. Display rate can still overstate responsiveness: with temporal
compression, several frames are decoded per predicted latent, so actions may enter less often than frames

on consumer hardware, accordingly reports rendered and latent FPS and control rate separately.
Few-step distillation and adversarial post-training. Training-based acceleration turns a many-step generator
into a causal student with fewer evaluations per frame or chunk, as CausVid, Self-Forcing, and Causal Forcing
do while aligning rollouts. Adversarial post-training goes further: Seaweed-APT [276] distills a video diffusion
model into a one-step generator, AAPT [277] applies autoregressive adversarial post-training to real-time
interactive video, and Causal Forcing++ [639] scales few-step autoregressive distillation. Once sampling depth
is reduced, the backbone, active context, and decoder dominate the cost, and the student must also stay
stable over long rollouts.
Training-free acceleration. Training-free methods exploit redundancy at inference. Sparse attention suits
streaming because the current query is short and the history long: Light Forcing [325] keeps the anchors
and recent frames that control the next output. Caching reuses features that change little across denoising
steps, as in TeaCache [281] and FasterCache [326]. WorldCache [107] adapts caching to world models, where
an error in a few control-sensitive action, image, or memory tokens can propagate through the rollout.
TurboDiffusion [617] combines both routes, stacking step distillation with low-bit and sparse attention and
weight–activation quantization, reporting two orders of magnitude of end-to-end acceleration on a single
consumer GPU.
7.2.4 Physical and Causal Grounding
Physical grounding asks whether motion and interaction respect contact, rigidity, gravity, and persistence.
Causal grounding asks how the predicted future changes when an action or initial condition is intervened on,
and whether unaffected factors stay fixed. Current methods offer much machinery for plausible dynamics,
whereas intervention-level supervision and evaluation remain less developed.
55


Video Foundation Models
World-Modeling Lens
Explicit physics simulation integration. Explicit methods let a simulator produce the dynamics and a
generator render them. PhysGen [294] and PhysMotion [434] estimate object shape and material from a single
image, simulate the motion, and use it to guide video generation. PhysGen3D [43] extends this to interactive
3D scenes with user-set initial conditions, and PhysCtrl [463] instead replaces the simulator with a generative
physics network trained on simulated animations, conditioned on material parameters and forces. Mass,
material, force, or initial velocity thus become editable, but deformable bodies, fluids, fracture, articulated
agents, and partly observed contact remain hard to recover from pixels and to simulate.
Implicit physics alignment. Implicit approaches keep a learned generator and change what it learns from.
VideoREPA [627] distills token-level relations from a self-supervised video encoder, which carries stronger
physical structure, into a text-to-video generator. Phantom [409] couples visual trajectories with auxiliary
physical latents, and RoboScape [407] trains with depth and temporal structure as additional targets.
PhyGDPO [37] post-trains on preferences between candidate videos ranked by physical plausibility, and
WROP [612] supplies procedurally generated Blender data for object permanence and solidity. Inference-time
planning instead adds no physics training: VLIPP [564] uses a vision-language model to plan coarse physical
motion and converts the plan into structured noise for a motion-controllable image-to-video diffusion model.
Intervention-level causal evidence. Plausibility objectives do not record which force, action, or initial
condition produced a motion. Causal evidence instead holds the initial state fixed, varies one action or physical
parameter, and compares both the affected future and the unchanged state. PhysEditWorld [173] supplies such
data by replaying identical game episodes under different gravity settings, with synchronized video, action,
engine-state, and parameter signals (see also Section 8.2). OneWorld [155] requires several action-conditioned
futures of one scene to share a latent physical explanation, scored by a mechanism interpreter during flow
training and sampling. Different actions should then change outcomes, not the world’s mechanism.
7.3 Applications
Applications differ in who consumes the prediction. A world model may produce a dataset, train a policy,
evaluate a policy, supply forecasts to a planner, or respond directly to a person. The first three support
decisions before deployment, whereas planning and human simulation consume predictions online. The
same action-conditioned generator can move between these roles as its control fidelity and temporal stability
improve.
7.3.1 Data Engines
As a data engine, a world model turns controllable variation into training examples where real observations are
expensive, dangerous, or unevenly distributed. Each example must include the labels the learner needs, often
actions. In robotics, DreamGen [198] and GigaWorld-0 [441] synthesize action-labeled trajectories for policy
training (Section 5.5). In driving, the action-conditioned generators of Section 7.1.2, such as GAIA-2 [399],
Vista [118], and Cosmos [355], supply unusual layouts and safety-critical cases that fleet logs rarely contain.
7.3.2 Policy Training in Imagination
Imagination training keeps the world model inside the optimization loop: a policy acts, the model predicts the
next observation or state, and a reward updates the policy. Unlike a data engine, the generated distribution
then follows the current policy, and errors compound along the trajectories it chooses. Action-conditioned
video models such as UniSim [561], iVideoGPT [513], and Pandora [525] can serve as this simulator if their
action effects, rewards, and resets are accurate enough.
World-VLA-Loop [297] refines vision-language-action policies by reinforcement learning in a world model that
predicts future frames and binary rewards. It feeds the updated policy’s rollouts back into the model, since a
fixed simulator fails most often on states that a changed policy visits. Dreamer 4 [147] trains a Minecraft agent
with reinforcement learning inside a video world model learned largely from offline video and action data.
Because each step of a diffusion world model may need many denoising evaluations, Horizon Imagination [74]
denoises several future observations in parallel and decouples the denoising budget from the rollout horizon,
making longer on-policy rollouts affordable.
56


Video Foundation Models
World-Modeling Lens
7.3.3 Policy Evaluation Environments
For policy evaluation, a world model replaces repeated deployment with reproducible generated trials.
WorldGym [386] rolls out robot policies in an action-conditioned video model and scores them with a vision-
language model, while WorldEval [259] encodes policy behavior with Policy2Vec to drive its simulation. Both
are validated by whether generated success rates preserve real-world policy rankings. Such agreement depends
on the policies tested, since a simulator tuned on familiar checkpoints may misrank a controller that visits
new states or exploits a modeling artifact.
Later systems add multi-view, memory-supported rollouts (Ctrl-World [140]) or task-progress and success
judges (GE-Sim 2.0 [385]), and generalist robot (DreamDojo [117]) and driving (GAIA-3 [496]) world models
list policy evaluation among their uses. OmniDreams [22] takes a hybrid route in driving, generating sensor
observations online inside the AlpaSim simulator, whose own services handle traffic and physics.
7.3.4 Predict-then-Act Planning
Predict-then-act methods use a forecast as evidence for the next decision and differ in how much of the
future they decode. At the pixel end, UniPi [87] and AVDC [215] generate a video plan and recover actions
with an inverse-dynamics model or dense correspondences (Section 5.5). Such plans are easy to inspect but
add generation cost and another source of error. Navigation World Models [18] instead predict egocentric
observations under candidate navigation actions with a conditional diffusion Transformer and plan by checking
which simulated trajectory reaches the goal. VPP [181] instead conditions an inverse-dynamics policy on
future-aware features from a fine-tuned video diffusion model. LaDi-WM [192] and MoWM [593] combine
latent prediction with feature or pixel signals, and LaWAM [51] predicts latent visual subgoals without
reconstructing video. At the latent end, V-JEPA 2-AC and DINO-WM score candidate actions without
decoding pixels, which cuts prediction cost but makes their forecasts harder to inspect.
Prediction can also serve only as training supervision. WLA-0 [565] trains shared representations with a
future-frame World Expert and an Action Expert, so the World Expert can be dropped at test time or
re-enabled to select among sampled action chunks.
7.3.5 Interactive Simulation for Humans
In human-interactive simulation, world prediction is the application: a person supplies actions, and the model
produces the evolving observation stream. Among playable systems, Genie made unlabeled video playable and
Genie 3 [367] opens text-prompted worlds to real-time exploration, GameNGen simulates an existing game
in real time, and Oasis [79] and Matrix-Game 2.0 [156] target real-time open deployment. Yume [337] and
RELIC [171] extend exploration to real-world scenes, with RELIC adding long-horizon memory for revisits.
Voice-controlled streaming characters such as Vidu S1 and Wan-Streamer (Section 7.2.3) extend the input from
game actions to speech, so response timing and turn-level coherence matter alongside visual continuity. In
driving, GAIA-2, Vista, and Cosmos-Drive-Dreams [393] let users set trajectories, actions, or scene conditions
and inspect the resulting futures. With a fixed-control model, the user edits conditions between rollouts,
whereas an interactive system accepts input within the stream.
7.4 Benchmarks and Evaluation
World-model evaluation follows the role of the prediction. A rendered future needs visual and motion quality,
and an interactive simulator adds action fidelity, persistent state, and bounded response time. Planning,
policy training, and policy evaluation further require the prediction to improve or faithfully estimate a
downstream decision. These requirements form the Interacting row of the evidence ladder in Table 2, from
visual plausibility through controllable rollout to decision utility [592]. Table 19 indexes current benchmarks
by the claim they test and the main limits on comparing their scores.
7.4.1 Challenge-Aligned Benchmarks
Action following and controllability. Action benchmarks test whether changing a control changes the predicted
future as intended. ACT-Bench [7] measures instruction–execution consistency and trajectory error for driving
