maneuvers, and iWorld-Bench [96] separates trajectory following from visual quality and memory through a
unified action layer. In robotics, MiraBench [563] tests action following, physical adherence, and optimism
under actions that should fail, which matters because a simulator that turns failed actions into plausible
successes misleads both policy training and evaluation. Since a game key, a camera pose, a robot action chunk,
and an ego trajectory need different error measures, reports should state the native action space, horizon,
intervention frequency, and source of trajectory estimates.
Long-horizon consistency and memory. Consistency asks how identity, geometry, and appearance degrade
with duration even when content stays visible. Measuring this degradation requires scores stratified by video
length. Memory tests instead remove content from view and check it later. Revisit benchmarks such as
LoopBench [520] test static recall, comparing returns with the model’s own first visit.
However, a static cache passes revisit tests even when the world should have changed. MBench [624] separates
entity, environment, and causal memory under occlusion and off-screen evolution, STEVO-Bench [331] hides
an ongoing process and checks whether it advanced, and MemoBench [49] requires an object that changed
out of view to return in its updated state. MIND [575] and WorldRoamBench [545] test memory during
closed-loop or open-world interaction, the latter scoring memory separately from action following, visual drift,
and controllability-gated physics. Memory Maze [368] poses the same retention question for navigation agents.
These protocols score decoded video; latent-state models lack matching probes of drift and goal reachability.
Real-time response. Latency is reported per system, usually as FPS or time to first response, and mixes
model forward time with denoising steps, decoding, cache construction, input rate, and hardware. A matched
protocol would measure the delay from an action’s arrival to the first affected frame, sustained throughput,
and latency variance over long sessions, at stated resolution, precision, hardware, and action frequency.
Physical, temporal, and causal validity. Physical evaluation ranges from prompt-based plausibility to compar-
ison with recorded outcomes. Prompt-based suites such as VideoPhy and VideoPhy-2 [16, 17] are described
in Section 5.1.2. PhyGenBench [341] organizes prompts by physical principle, and WorldModelBench [241]
combines instruction following with checks for violations such as broken conservation. Physics-IQ [346] instead
58


Video Foundation Models
World-Modeling Lens
compares continuations of a real initial segment with recorded outcomes, and PhyWorldBench [137] adds
anti-physics prompts that test whether a model can follow instructions that deliberately violate physics while
staying logically consistent. Other suites probe specific factors. WorldBench [458] separates intuitive principles
from quantities such as friction and viscosity, and CRONOS [24] varies viewpoint, scene, appearance, and
category within controlled events. What-If World [34] pairs prompts that change one physical detail and scores
both the outcome difference and the preservation of the environment. ChronoMagic-Bench [598] tests temporal
direction through metamorphic and time-lapse processes such as growth or construction. Prompt-level and
rendered contrasts cover only part of the intervention protocol of Section 7.2.4, which ultimately needs logged
actions and real outcomes.
Calibration of predicted futures. Stochastic world models should also produce the right spread of futures.
PAWBench [376] and CaliBench [400] sample repeated futures from a fixed input and compare the outcome
distribution with reference probabilities or sets of valid outcomes, reporting separately how often outcomes
can be scored. C3 [339] calibrates confidence rather than outcome frequencies: it trains a probe that predicts
whether a latent prediction is accurate and evaluates it against accuracy labels.
7.4.2 Application-Aligned Benchmarks
Application-aligned benchmarks measure a prediction’s effect after another component consumes it, through
policy-ranking agreement, optimization gains, planning success, executable action success, or data efficiency.
For policy evaluation, WorldGym [386] and WorldEval [259] compare model-based success rates or rankings
with real robot trials. WMBench, introduced with GigaWorld-1 [128], studies evaluator design with an ordinal
score that separates correct from incorrect task outcomes and high from low visual fidelity. For planning,
WorldPrediction [45] tests high-level state transitions and procedural ordering, using counterfactual distractors
that remove low-level continuity shortcuts. World-in-World [614] places heterogeneous models behind a shared
action interface and measures closed-loop task success.
Embodied benchmarks make predictions executable or check them against task requirements. RoboWM-
Bench [201] converts generated manipulation videos into actions and executes them in a physics simulator, so
visually plausible but unexecutable motion earns little credit. EWMBench [600] separates scene consistency,
motion correctness, and semantic alignment, showing that visually stable output can miss the task motion.
WorldLens [267] and DrivingGen [658] evaluate driving models, from generation and reconstruction to action
following, trajectory plausibility, and downstream tasks. WorldArena [406] places perceptual quality alongside
data-engine, policy-evaluation, and planning utility and finds that the two rankings need not agree.
Latent-state models need their own audits. ARC-Bench [634] tests whether frozen latent-distance objectives
rank candidate actions by their executed outcome costs, reporting rank agreement and regret while varying
the replanning frequency, since frequent replanning can mask poor scoring. PokeWorld [433] pushes visually
identical objects with different mass, drag, and stiffness to ask whether these parameters can be decoded from
the latent state and whether predicted action effects depend on them correctly.
7.4.3 Foundational and Comprehensive Benchmarks
Foundational video benchmarks set the visual and temporal quality floor on which control, memory, and
utility tests build. They include the generation suites VBench and VBench-2.0 [193, 647] (Section 5.1.2) and
VBench++ [194], which adds image-conditioned generation and trustworthiness.
Comprehensive world-model suites differ mainly in protocol. WorldScore [89] asks models to generate successive
scenes along given camera trajectories and scores controllability, quality, and dynamics. WorldSimBench [383]
pairs human-aligned perceptual assessment with a manipulation test that turns generated video into control
signals. WorldMark [546] and WBench [583] evaluate interactive generation through standardized action
sequences and multi-turn episodes, respectively. WorldOlympiad [645] scores physical faithfulness, geometric
consistency, and interaction fidelity across games, robotics, and open-domain video. Shared tooling helps hold
data and controllers fixed across comparisons: the stable-worldmodel platform [333] provides common data
interfaces, baselines, planners, and environments with controllable visual, geometric, and physical factors.
59


Video Foundation Models
World-Modeling Lens
­ INSIGHT From predictive state to intervention and utility
Evidence for a world model should match its transition protocol (Table 2). Passive models show predictive
structure, and controlled models must also change the world where an action acts and keep what it leaves
untouched. Interactive systems must preserve those consequences under feedback, and decision applications must
turn them into better choices.
8
Open Challenges and Future Directions
Seven open problems span reading, writing, sharing, and interacting; each needs new methods and better tests.
Current models share recurring limits: encoders rarely maintain a predictive state, generators lose identity
and physical constraints over time, unified models have yet to show controlled synergy, and interactive
systems degrade as generated history accumulates. These limits, together with gaps in evaluation, action
supervision, generalization, and uncertainty, define seven challenges: long-horizon consistency, physical and
causal grounding, measurable synergy, standardized evaluation, control from unlabeled video, generalization
across scenes and embodiments, and stochastic worlds with rare events.
8.1 Long-Horizon World Consistency
Long-horizon consistency requires a model to retrieve distant evidence, preserve state that is no longer visible,
and render that state consistently in later predictions. Long-video understanding benchmarks [151, 472, 509,
654] mainly test the first of these (Section 4.1). Autoregressive generation and interaction add a further
difficulty: generated errors enter the history that later predictions depend on. Current methods balance recent
and distant context [313, 619], store retrievable observations [171, 528], or compress history into recurrent or
fixed-size memory [26, 502] (Section 7.2.2). However, most of them store what was visible, whereas a predictive
state must also track what changed off-screen; only a few systems model such change explicitly [90, 465]. For
example, a hidden ice cube keeps its identity as it melts (Figure 11). Replaying the stored observation returns
a stale cube [49].
Figure 11 Hidden state evolves during occlusion; replaying an old observation
can miss that change.
One method direction is memory
that updates a hidden state dur-
ing occlusion, trained to predict
how unseen entities evolve, instead
of replaying stored frames.
Such
memory complements rollout train-
ing: LIVE [187] targets error growth
through forward–reverse cycle consis-
tency without any external memory,
so the two address different failures
and can be combined. Memory must
also fit an interactive budget: un-
capped archives grow with the roll-
out, and retention budgets [1] turn
this growth into eviction decisions,
so learning what to keep is itself a
research problem.
Memory benchmarks [49, 545, 575,
624] now test revisits, occlusion, and
off-screen change (Section 7.4). The
next step is to vary rollout length
and revisit distance separately and report degradation curves instead of one terminal score, so that rendering
drift and state loss can be told apart. Latent-state models have no frames to score, so they need probes of
60


Video Foundation Models
World-Modeling Lens
goal reachability, feature stability, and decision quality as the horizon grows. Consistency results should also
report memory size, end-to-end latency, and action-update rate.
8.2 Physical and Causal Grounding
Figure 12 Changing gravity alters the trajectory while unrelated scene prop-
erties remain fixed.
Physical grounding can be tested at
three increasingly demanding levels.
Reading benchmarks [23, 395, 578] test
whether a model recognizes persistence,
stability, contact, and counterfactual
outcomes in observed events.
Gener-
ation benchmarks [16, 17, 137, 241, 341,
598] ask whether a synthesized outcome
follows physical commonsense, a named
law, or the direction of an irreversible
process (Section 5.1.2).
Action- and
parameter-conditioned models face a
stronger test: changing an action or
physical parameter should change the af-
fected outcome and leave unrelated state
intact. For example, a ball released with
the same velocity under two gravity set-
tings should follow different trajectories
while the camera and background stay
fixed (Figure 12). A realistic continua-
tion can still respond wrongly to gravity or alter unrelated state, so realism is not enough [173, 241].
Current methods add physics at different stages (Section 7.2.4): they align generator features with a video
encoder [627], simulate dynamics before rendering [294], condition generation on a planned motion [564],
reward geometric consistency [473], or retrieve videos of similar phenomena [69]. Each depends on an outside
source: the teacher’s coverage, the recovered state and material parameters, the generator’s ability to follow
the plan, or the quality of the feedback and retrieved evidence. Paired rollouts that change one physical
parameter [173], production data with recorded branches [36], and coupled action-conditioned futures [155]
provide the missing contrast between a change and its effect. One direction combines these physics priors
with intervention-paired data, with objectives that reward the intended change and penalize drift elsewhere.
Evaluation should also diagnose failures: an arrow-of-time error, a contact violation, and a wrong response to
an action all make a clip implausible, but they implicate different model components and training signals.
Recent intervention benchmarks [24, 34, 458] point toward a common protocol: hold the initial state fixed,
change one action or physical parameter, and test the changed outcome, the invariance of unrelated state,
the first violated constraint, and when it appears. Applying the same interventions to reading questions,
generated continuations, and closed-loop rollouts would make transfer across operations measurable.
8.3 Measurable Synergy in Unified Video Models
Unified video models span discrete-token, continuous, and modular designs [306, 479, 486, 498, 609], and
some route information from generation to understanding, from reasoning to generation, or into planning [310,
362, 382] (Sections 6.1 and 6.2.4). The open question is causal: how much of the gain comes from shared
representations or information flow, and how much from additional data, parameters, or supervision? Video
evidence of transfer is so far partial or one-directional: video-diffusion features support recognition and
geometry [213, 468], an MLLM’s reasoning trace raises generation scores [362], mixing data helps both
tasks [111], a frozen vision-language critic improves editing [298], and predicted features affect actions [466]
(Section 6.2). Breadth suites [257, 501] catch regressions but not transfer, and image-domain synergy tests [412,
562] miss what video adds: transferred information must preserve temporal order, motion, identity, and
evolving state.
61


Video Foundation Models
World-Modeling Lens
Figure 13 Two systems with matched resources differ only in whether
information passes between understanding and generation.
On the method side, each transfer path
can be built as a separate component that
can be switched off, such as a temporal-
analysis loss on the generator or generated
intermediate states passed to a temporal
reasoner. Round-trip consistency between
inferred trajectories and rendered video can
then serve as a training signal as well as a
test.
The test itself compares two systems with
the same tasks, data, capacity, and com-
pute that differ only in whether the cross-
direction path is enabled (Figure 13). Tem-
poral analysis should then improve event or-
der, motion, identity, or physical dynamics
in generation, and generated intermediate
states should improve temporal inference or
planning under the same observation and
parameter budget. Round trips render an inferred trajectory or event graph and read it back, or analyze,
revise, and regenerate a rollout. Each task must define its invariants, because one description can match
several valid videos, and the intermediate representation must not carry the answer verbatim [353].
8.4 Standardized Evaluation of World Properties
Figure 14 Video and feature predictions need different probes,
but both face the same decision test.
World-model evaluation now covers control, mem-
ory, physical validity, and decision utility as well as
visual realism, but the resulting numbers are rarely
comparable. Suites for generated video, action re-
sponse, and broader world properties [7, 89, 96,
193, 267, 545, 546, 583, 645, 647] differ in prompts,
control spaces, horizons, and judges (Section 7.4),
so scores do not transfer across suites. For example,
two predictors guiding the same robot may return
video and features, respectively (Figure 14). Video
allows visual and trajectory checks while features
need state probes, and both need a decision test,
because either can score well on its native metric
yet fail at control [592, 634].
A reporting standard should first match the proto-
col to the model’s prediction space and transition
interface (Section 3.4). Interactive models need
mid-rollout interventions and latency tests, action-conditioned models need faithful responses to fixed controls,
and passive predictors need held-out transition tests. Each result should state the horizon, action frequency,
conditioning, and judge model, and claims should rise from visual to controllable-rollout to decision-utility
evidence [592] (Table 2). Real-time claims need control-to-display latency, sustained throughput, and latency
variance, reported with hardware, resolution, denoising steps, and active context, because frames per second
alone is ambiguous.
Shared infrastructure [333] standardizes data readers, planning solvers, and environment variations. Fixed-
candidate action ranking should accompany closed-loop success, because frequent replanning can hide a poor
scorer [634]. Integrity needs held-out environments and leakage-controlled synthetic tasks, because web-scale
and synthetic training data make overlap hard to detect. On the method side, two tools need development in
their own right: learned judges, which can inherit their source models’ blind spots and need validation against
human ratings, and standard probes that read latent predictions without a pixel decoder.
62


Video Foundation Models
World-Modeling Lens
8.5 Scaling Control from Unlabeled Video
Figure 15 Action effects mix with camera and background motion.
Internet video covers objects, agents,
and dynamics but usually lacks
the action behind each transition.
Latent-action methods [32, 61, 403,
574] infer actions as a learned vari-
able (Section 7.2.1), enabling the
use of far more video than labeled
datasets, but scale alone does not
specify what the variable represents.
For example, a gripper may push a
block while the camera pans and a
windmill turns (Figure 15).
Only
the push is the agent’s action, yet
a latent code trained for reconstruc-
tion can absorb the camera and back-
ground motion while reproducing the
clip. Distractor experiments confirm
this and show that limited action su-
pervision can substantially improve
the recovered factors [351].
Reconstruction accuracy and codebook usage are thus weak evidence of control: a useful latent action should
predict controllable effects, stay stable under camera and background changes, and transfer through a simple
mapping. Several weak signals can push latent actions toward these properties. A small labeled set can
anchor latent dimensions to real controls, multi-view invariance can separate camera motion from scene
change, temporal bottlenecks can discard details irrelevant to control, and paired interventions can mark
which changes belong to the agent. Continuous latent actions [119], human–robot shared representations [57],
and egomotion controls from tracked pixel motion [430] move in this direction, while state-annotated data [36,
264] give stronger but narrower supervision.
Pseudo-action generation offers another source of training pairs: a video model produces a trajectory and
an inverse or latent-action model labels its controls. Such data helps only when the inferred action has the
intended effect in a controlled environment or real system; otherwise the generator and the labeller reinforce
each other’s shortcuts. Evaluation should keep a labeled subset with interventions and measure transfer under
unseen cameras, backgrounds, scenes, and embodiments.
8.6 Generalization across Scenes and Embodiments
Figure 16 A held-out scene–body pair tests whether familiar
factors compose while preserving action effects.
Scene generalization changes appearance, layout, ob-
jects, or local dynamics for the same body, as when a
robot enters a new room. Embodiment generalization
changes the body, sensors, action frequency, degrees
of freedom, or physical meaning of an action, as when
skills move from human hands to a robot arm.
Zero-shot claims therefore depend on which factor is
held out. V-JEPA 2-AC [9] transfers to new scenes
without target data, but on a robot type seen in
post-training, not an unseen embodiment.
Other
methods adapt latent actions with limited interac-
tion [119], generate target-domain experience [198,
441], or broaden the pretraining prior [587]. For ex-
ample, training may cover a human hand in a lab
and a kitchen but a robot only in the lab (Figure 16).
Deploying the robot in the kitchen tests an unseen
63


Video Foundation Models
World-Modeling Lens
scene–body pair, where the representation and control mapping must still move the block to the target.
Cross-embodiment transfer adds an alignment problem: the same semantic action may need different joints
and timing on different bodies, while similar-looking motion can have different contact consequences. Current
methods connect human and robot behavior through shared image-goal units [57] or map latent actions to
robot commands with a small labeled set [574]. A method direction is to train such mappings to preserve
effects, with objectives defined on outcomes such as object displacement, contact, and task completion instead
of raw control values or token similarity. Evaluation should cross scenes, embodiments, sensors, and action
spaces and test effect equivalence, controllability, and safety after transfer. Reports should state whether
results are zero-shot, calibrated, or fully fine-tuned, and how much target data they used.
8.7 Stochastic Worlds and Rare Events
One history can admit several valid futures, because other agents may act differently and relevant physical
state may be hidden. A deterministic predictor cannot express these alternatives, and squared-error training
can favor an average of incompatible outcomes. Latent-variable predictors [11, 230] sample multiple futures,
and controllable generators and data engines [118, 198, 355, 399, 441] can synthesize rare scenarios for training
(Section 7). However, synthesis does not say how likely each outcome is: a prompted collision supplies a
training case, whereas a calibrated collision risk supports a decision under uncertainty. For example, given a
fixed driving history and ego actions, a pedestrian may wait, cross after the car passes, or step into its path
(Figure 17). A generator can render the rare crossing without assigning it a reliable probability.
Figure 17 Valid futures differ in frequency, and invalid and unreadable samples
must be counted separately.
One direction is to develop genera-
tors that assign calibrated outcome
probabilities rather than merely pro-
duce diverse samples, using empiri-
cal event rates or justified simulator
priors. Generated rare cases must
be checked for physical feasibility,
causal relevance, and downstream
utility before entering training data,
since prompting fixes which failures
appear but not their frequencies.
For a fixed history and action se-
quence, distributional evaluation as-
sesses whether samples cover dis-
tinct valid outcomes, exclude invalid
ones, and match observed or simu-
lated outcome frequencies.
Diver-
sity alone rewards arbitrary varia-
tion, and best-of-many scoring hides
misplaced probability mass. Reports
should therefore use proper scoring rules, calibration curves, and mode coverage at a fixed sample budget,
distinguish unreadable from invalid samples, and use sample sizes appropriate to event rarity. Repeated-
sampling benchmarks [376, 400] and confidence calibration [339] are a start (Section 7.4), but extending
outcome-frequency tests from endpoints in limited scenarios to long, action-conditioned trajectories is open.
­ INSIGHT Scaling world models needs interventions, not just more clips
Passive video expands coverage but leaves hidden state, physical causes, cross-direction transfer, action semantics,
and outcome probabilities underdetermined. Most of these challenges need data and protocols with interventions
(known hidden changes, paired physical edits, matched ablations, labeled actions, held-out scene–body pairs, and
observed event rates). Observational scale shows what co-occurs; interventions show what changes what.
64


Video Foundation Models
World-Modeling Lens
9
Conclusion
This survey has treated video as an observed trace of a latent temporal world and used
Y reading,
Ò writing,
T sharing, and
 interacting to organize how foundation models connect observations to represented state.
Comprehension recovers what a trace reveals; generation constructs traces from conditions; unification shares
information and machinery between the two; and world modeling asks how represented state changes under
conditions or actions. Within this framing, a video world model is defined by the transition it learns, which
predicts future video or video-derived features and runs passively, under a fixed action sequence, or interactively
(Section 3.4). The distinction between temporal coherence and world consistency (Definition 2.4) separates
the quality of rendered video from evidence that a model tracks the world state behind it. The next phase
demands stronger evidence (Table 2). Beyond perceptual fidelity and local coherence, claims about the world
require state that survives occlusion, revisits, and long horizons; controllable action effects; valid responses
to interventions; and, for closed-loop use, latency within the control budget. Stochastic futures also require
coverage and calibration, unified models require matched evidence that information transfers between reading
and writing, and systems built for training, planning, or evaluation must improve decisions in controlled
environments or real tasks. Meeting these requirements would move the field from rendering convincing video
to modeling how the world changes.
